### Cost of finding a diagnostic spoken example

Freeze a preview-budget transfer test on the two previous voices and folds. Shuffle the available 225-wpm public examples into 128 matched stream orderings (seed 171001 + fold, one RNG per fold); consider first 0, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64 or all rows per fold. Query the previous balanced-disagreement selector on this prefix, maximum four labels. Charge .25 for an answer and .01 per second of distinct audio previewed per fold, with identical waveforms under different conventions cached. For each target choose ONE budget maximizing the other voice's expected net on opened 213-wpm tests, smaller on ties. Freeze budget before fresh 247-wpm synthesis. Compare to skip, exhaustive preview, and uniform random labels at equal previews and answer count; report descriptive budget frontier and separately a +1 net unit over both skip and random across 150 outputs per voice as primary. Use the old strings, and treat stream permutations as availability-order sensitivity, not independent speakers. Time audio from actual files and distinguish modeled fee from compute. If the source selects skip or full scanning, retain its choice.

Before opening the 247-wpm scores, add a horizon sensitivity check. Repeat the same output distribution four times, holding lesson costs fixed, and fit the budget on the other voice's 213-wpm rows under this horizon. This is a modeled amortization check on reused jobs; count fresh questions and uncertainty only once. Require both transferred policies to select positive budgets and gain ≥1 total net unit over skip at the longer horizon. Freeze this choice before scoring either target.

Source-only selection at the original horizon chose zero previews on both voices. At 247 wpm this yielded 145/150 Samantha, 142/150 Joelle, tying skip and matched random and missing the +1 primary margin. Hindsight best tested budget was zero for Samantha and exhaustive for Joelle. Exhaustive preview took 188.91/195.74 seconds of distinct stored audio summed across the two deployments per voice (physical recording duration, not wall-clock inference time); mean labels 6.00/6.43. At the short horizon exhaustive minus skip net −.39/+.87; maximum affordable price per audio second at fixed .25 label fee .00794/.01446. Every smaller positive budget tested lost to skip on average at the preregistered price. Margins and decisions were replayed locally from registered acoustic features; pools contained 135/141 public presentations and reused some waveforms.

At the fourfold modeled horizon the opposite-voice fits both chose exhaustive preview. Fresh gains in cumulative net over skip +8.61/+14.19, and over same-cost random teaching +11.53/+17.00. Both met the +1 point-estimate criterion. Frame-paired 95% bootstrap intervals conditional on the learned offsets and average order-specific charges were [−3.39,20.61] and [1.31,30.19] vs skip; ten frames per fold. Fourfold reuse extrapolates the same job mix and the 128 availability orders quantify tie/subset variability. It does not constitute 600 distinct recorded decisions or a measurement of power or inference latency. Independent arithmetic reconstructed every budget/order net from raw decisions, and all 78 recorded strings per voice differed bytewise from their training-rate waveforms. ### Grounding which participant a spoken question asks about

Freeze a two-intention question test. The same report denotes a person in a known conversational exchange. Two question forms request that person's identity or the identity of their conversational partner. A single known particle at the beginning or end sounds the difference; two counterfactual communities assign it opposite meanings. Supply the two candidate answer operations, the existing report semantics, anonymous person handles and partner links. Fit a separate meaning for each heard position using two pointed demonstrations in total, one per form, on the same teaching base with a different cast from testing. Withhold target names/orders as complete bases. Evaluate 12 target bases (two new name/marker/cue triples under all six permutations), both particle positions and both communities, on four fresh scenes per base. Identical audio/scenes across communities, matched base within the form swap. Freeze seed 171301, 197/239 wpm teaching/test, Samantha and Joelle. Primary ≥80% sets with all four answers correct on each voice, ≥20-point advantage over crossed teacher pointers. Also score exact tokens, omitted teaching (explicit unresolved), always answer the report, and form-only ignoring report content. Count distinct recordings separately from scene replays. Use class-constrained acoustic hearing allowing all four particle positions and all base orders; verify sample costs through registered connected scoring. This tests grounded question focus within an explicit finite grammar.

Separately challenge a form-only strategy by flipping just the report cue 5↔6 in each held base, with the same scene and identical question construction. Preserve both fits; require ≥75% complete sets across both report variants, both question forms and both communities. The two intended answers exchange when the cue flips. This paired test removes incidental advantages from choosing a fixed participant.

Each voice heard both teaching recordings exactly. On 12 held base orders × four scenes, complete sets for heard / exact / crossed pointer / no teaching / always report / fixed teaching-report content: Samantha 48/48/0/0/0/24 and Joelle 44/48/0/0/0/24 (all out of 48). Heard individual answers 192/192 and 184/192. All untrained focus readings deferred. Both learned the opposite position-to-operation mapping when only the pointers changed. Both heard 23/24 original held questions exactly. Samantha reordered content words once without changing the referenced person; Joelle substituted one participant name, causing all four scene-set failures. The 24 cue-flipped recordings were heard exactly per voice. Complete eight-answer sets under both report cues, marker positions and teaching conventions: 48/48 and 44/48; any answer invariant to the report-cue change must fail every set. Both point-estimate thresholds passed. Four scene replays and two teacher conventions share the same recordings; there are only two lexical triples before the cue flip. No independent population estimate.

The semantic learner accessed pointed individuals and the heard form; two answer operations, partner links, question particle identity and report semantics are supplied. It chose an answer by pointing, with no spoken answer-generation test or economic claim. Independently recomputed the targets from the two participants and verified the matched teaching interventions and waveform candidate scores. External question_focus.py VOICE, question_focus_swap.py VOICE and question_focus_check.py under mac.sh reproduce the study. The local question-focus-pair-samantha.wav contains two matched recordings: with the original teaching, initial vs final “teka” requests person 3 vs person 1 in that scene; reversing the two teaching pointers reverses the answers.

### Answering a participant question aloud across privately grounded name banks

Freeze a spoken-answer extension of the focus test on its 48 known scene/base combinations and two counterfactual conventions per question voice. In each direction the other synthetic voice answers. The answerer and final listener independently receive two ambiguous visual demonstrations per one-word answer name (true person plus a different distractor each time). Use two arbitrary eight-name lexicons related by a fixed-point-free permutation. Candidate utterances for the answerer are eight unlabeled audio playback buttons in random order. Learn production by matching each button's sound to grounded demonstrations and select by the privately inferred person. The final listener receives only the response waveform, with its own bank trained on that partner's two named scenes; no outgoing button ID, question, convention or scene. Supply word turn boundaries, persistent person handles and a one-word/one-person stable lexicon; do not supply the receiver an identity transcript. Fit each arm from its own exposures. Treat both teacher rates as one example each, not multiple speakers. Preregister ≥80% of scene sets correct across both question forms, question conventions and reply lexicons in each direction, and ≥20 points joint loss for changing only the receiver's lexicon. Include exact private pointer, exact emitted word, mismatched and withheld reply controls. Test 155/183-wpm answerer lessons, 173/201-wpm receiver lessons, 237-wpm playback buttons. Freeze random seed 171401 and compare with hearing only the first ambiguous demonstration. Keep mixed or negative evidence; account separately for report comprehension, production and reply hearing.

Samantha questions with Joelle replies passed 48/48 complete scene sets (384/384 individual). Joelle questions with Samantha replies passed 44/48 (368/384). Supplying exact question comprehension recovered 48/48 in the latter; supplying exact emission alone kept 44/48. The crossed receiver lexicon and substituted word each gave 0/48 complete sets per direction (0/384 or 2/384 individual); silence gave 0/384. In all four reply voice/lexicon combinations the answerer associated every playback button correctly and the receiver grounded 8/8 test words. After the first eight ambiguous lessons even an exhaustive bijection solver had two valid assignments differing on every word. After both rounds a separate receiver process reproduced all 32 voice/lexicon/word judgments using only PCM waveforms in anonymized WAV containers and candidate visual pairs for teaching; shuffled test waveforms carried no filenames, query, scene or intended referent. This verifies the isolated reply channel. Each fresh receiver saw 16 visual/acoustic examples, and each producer saw 16 separately. Across conditions the spoken reply inventory uses only eight physical 237-wpm recordings per voice. The test builds on archived private question hearings and uses pre-recorded one-word buttons for production. Scene and lexicon forks are paired, not independent dialogues with human speakers. External spoken_focus.py QUESTION_VOICE REPLY_VOICE and spoken_focus_wire.py QUESTION_VOICE REPLY_VOICE under mac.sh reproduce each direction. The example spoken-focus-dialogue.wav contains two Samantha questions answered by Joelle: “kemi” for person 3 and “teka” for person 1 under the first reply lexicon.

### When one pointed answer teaches the meanings of other spoken names

Generate anonymous candidate pairs first, then sample their meanings. Compare one connected ring of eight ambiguous words with disconnected rings of lengths six and two. On each ring a bijective assignment has two possible orientations; unconstrained assignments independently choose either candidate for every word, permitting synonyms. Both mechanisms expose identical candidate pairs and audio before inquiry. A learner is supplied these two hypotheses, with a mixture weight estimated from the proportion of bijections in 16 settled same-community vocabularies, correcting for accidental bijections under independent choices. No target regime label. Use seed 171501, 32 source-history draws per community (true structural weights .125/.875); randomize word and person identifiers, pair and presentation order. Ground eight anonymous acoustic clusters from 173-wpm ambiguous lessons, score 251-wpm independent renditions on both voices. Provide an eight-word upcoming usage inventory as audio only, one output per word, in shuffled order. Allow one teacher pointer at a price of 1.5 correct outputs, or skip. Use expected accuracy with fair credit for unresolved two-way guesses and a common scoring prior for all policies (true community mixture only in evaluator). Compare learned, forced one-to-one, forced independent, always buy, never buy, and random question. Primary: for the single ring with a bijective public contract, one receipt must resolve all eight words under both orientations on both voices. For the disconnected rings, require learned policy to query the larger ring on ≥80% of rich-history placements and skip ≥80% in the low-regularity community, and outperform best fixed buy/skip by ≥.15 net per eight outputs equally weighting the two communities. Report held uncertainty and losses on synonym exceptions, alternate receipt, costs of source vocabularies and acoustic scope. This tests transfer under supplied structural hypotheses.

Both listeners identified all eight 251-wpm words from the 173-wpm templates. On a single ring under the explicit bijective contract, the eight ambiguous exposures and one further pointer uniquely resolved all eight names under both assignments in every layout (64 layouts per voice). The orientation assumption is decisive: with the same public prefix and independent meanings, the other seven choices remain at probability .5. On disconnected rings, all 32 high-regularity history draws selected a name in the six-word component, and all 32 low-regularity draws skipped. Across the balanced community mix the resulting expected net per eight outputs was 4.594 vs 4.245 always buy under the fitted prior, 4.250 for unconditional bijective inference plus best purchase, 4.000 skip and 3.996 random purchase. Gain over the stronger unconditional control +.344. Mean net by community was 5.188 high and 4 low. Reversing the sole paid pointer in the high condition cost 5.375 correct outputs on average at equal fees. On genuinely nonbijective exceptions inside that community, net fell to 2.960 versus 4 for skipping. Each historical vocabulary costs eight settled associations; 128 prior associations per installation excluded from the target net. The two voices share the exact same simulated histories, not two independent semantic replications.

Evaluator integrated all 256 assignments; target expected reward credits ties at one half. History-only paired bootstrap interval on the gain over max(always buy under the fitted prior, skip) [.344,.358]; the gain over fixed hard-bijection purchase is .344 for every disconnected-ring history pair in this sample. In the single-ring mixture, learned net 5.022 vs 4.750 hard purchase and 4 skip. An independent check recovered each purchase from its analytic gain (.5 times one plus the estimated regularity times the number of *other* names linked by the same orientation, minus the 1.5 fee). For a connected component of size six, asking pays in expectation when the regularity probability exceeds .4. A post hoc calibration check under a forced pointer gave mean Brier loss .247/.060 for a second linked name in the low/high communities, compared with .438/.063 assuming a bijection and .250/.250 assuming independence. Only lexical frequency was estimated; the hypotheses, applicability of the structural prior and exact posterior decision algorithm were supplied. Registered acoustic matching ran locally; target meanings, simulated teaching costs and the eight-output inventory were evaluated in external replay. External lex_scope.py VOICE and lex_scope_check.py under mac.sh reproduce the saved outcomes.

### Learning a lexical relation on two names and applying it across eight

Source examples consist only of two heard names with overlapping candidate pairs and both final pointers. Across 12 examples per community randomize people, sound handles, pair order, and the phase of the two pointers (balanced). Learn an empirical table indexed by whether the known word designated the shared person, predicting whether the neighbor designates that person. These equality tests and repeated application to adjacent words are supplied; no bijection, synonym flag, or list of community conventions enters the fit. Compare a community whose adjacent choices select the shared person on exactly one side with one where they agree. Freeze seed 171601, 32 held layouts per voice, both anchor values and both communities on identical target layouts. With only one grounded target word, predict meanings four hops away in an eight-word cycle. Primary ≥85% complete sets across both anchor values per community/voice, ≥30 points over stopping propagation after one hop, and ≥90% correct at distance four. Also test two disconnected four-word rings, local renaming, withholding/inverting the anchor, a forced one-to-one control, and exhaustive assignment checking with the same learned relation. With the same source and start, score a hidden target exception separately as an identifiability limit. Ground test audio through registered isolated matching at 259 wpm from 173-wpm exemplars. Report acoustic versus combinatorial replication and assistance supplied.

Both voices matched 8/8 source and 8/8 fresh-rate isolated forms. The two empirical tables learned opposite answers to the same observable question: if my neighbor designates the shared person, do I designate them too? On eight-word cycles all 32/32 layouts per community and voice recovered the complete meanings under BOTH anchor choices; one-step propagation achieved 0/32, exhaustive assignment with the learned relation matched throughout, and the hard bijection achieved 32/32 in the first community and 0/32 in the second. Four-hop endpoints were correct 64/64 per community, but both conventions agree at even distances on this design. The diagnostic distant contrast was three hops: learned 128/128 versus hard bijection 0/128 in the second community. Independent word/participant relabeling and shuffled pair/presentation order preserved the entire solution. In two separated rings, all four anchored words resolved and all four others remained unresolved in every fork. Inverting the sole pointer reversed the entire predicted component, with no complete successes. A hidden change of just the farthest name preserved every available input and produced 0/64 correct exception meanings per community. Abstention on an unconnected name and vulnerability to a hidden exception are separate outcomes.

Each source community used 12 two-name scenes and 24 grounded pointers. Numbers above use the same simulated assignments in both acoustic conditions; each target voice contributed eight test WAVs, reused across placements. The implementation supplies stable names, exactly two candidate individuals per word, the shared-person comparison and the repeated propagation algorithm. The fitted local conditional table generalizes to longer variable bindings in this artificial vocabulary. No grammar induction or new natural-language vocabulary is inferred from this result. Registered acoustic operations ran locally, with semantic inference in external lex_neighbor.py VOICE and independent lex_neighbor_check.py under mac.sh. Saved source pairs, fitted tables, all assignments and hearing diagnostics accompany both scripts.

### Productive number marking within a spoken word

Test a small morphological construction. Teach eight bare names with a single pictured individual per kind. In each of two disjoint four-stem teaching folds, show same-kind singleton/group contrasts for the four source stems only. Mark both counts overtly with nonce endings “ka”/“mo”, or in a separate language prepend them; reverse their meanings in matched teacher forks. Synthesize each inflected word as one unspaced string. The listener receives registered speech-envelope frames and kind/count demonstrations, no spelling, boundary, affix template, or true edge. Supply a hypothesis of a stem and an edge marker, two grounded counts, and search over either edge and variable split locations. Fit splits to match known bare-stem sound and select the edge using leave-one-source-stem-out prediction. Use 185-wpm source forms, 223-wpm held forms, 173-wpm bare stems, split by alternating word indices in the existing eight-name bank. Require ≥6/8 held stems with correct kind AND count on both forms under both meanings for each voice/edge; compare whole-word neighbor, audio-duration-only number with same stem reader, crossed pointers and oracle stem/edge diagnostics. Count performance separately from stem failures. No natural speech, noun-class discovery or spontaneous segmentation claim.

On four-way held-stem sets (both inflections under both independently refitted teaching conventions), prefix/suffix scores were 7/8 and 5/8 for Samantha, 5/8 and 6/8 for Joelle. The ≥6 criterion passed in two of four cells. Source-only selection located the true marker edge in all eight voice/edge/fold fits. On prefixes both voices retained the correct kind for all 16 held recordings, but number was right on 15/16 and 13/16. On suffixes both heard number correctly on 16/16, but kind on only 12/16 and 14/16. Supplying the intended kind resolved all suffix cases and did nothing for prefix number. Switching only the grounded teacher counts and refitting flipped every held number judgment with all held kinds unchanged. Duration alone yielded 0/8 complete pairs in every cell. Whole-word nearest-source number plus whole-wave bare-template kind yielded prefix/suffix 5/0 Samantha and 6/0 Joelle; it too recognized 16/16 suffix counts per voice. Thus suffix count recognition alone does not identify a benefit of segmentation. Joint segmentation improved retaining which kind was named with suffixes, but the simple whole-word comparator won a prefix cell.

Each fit received eight bare kind examples and eight inflected kind/count examples from four stems, with the other four stems held out in both inflections. Teaching supplied count labels equivalent to pictured singleton/group outcomes; there was no image recognition in the experiment. Two label forks share all physical waveforms. The eight nonce stems and the two synthetic voices limit generalisation. Independent check verified both refits, held pair counts and distinct rate files. External morph_number.py VOICE prepare/score/reverse, morph_number_check.py and morph_number_replay.py under mac.sh reproduce the recordings, selections and diagnostic. The local listening example morph-number-listening.wav presents pavoka/pavomo, followed by the bare held name zilu and its two inflections.

### Transferring a single marked name to its unheard plural

Freeze eight new target stems (navi, dupo, gavi, rulo, biko, tavi, nelo, saro). Use the earlier eight stems with both overt suffixes at 185 wpm as a common source, preserving the verified suffix-side reader. Enroll all target bare forms at 173 wpm. In two installations “ka” or “mo” marks singular. Compare no extra exposure, one 201-wpm bare exemplar per name, and one 201-wpm marked singular per name. Estimate the marked lesson's cut by minimizing the summed distances to the known bare template and to the previously grounded singular suffix bank; add only its proposed stem interval as one further pronunciation for that kind. Keep all original templates. No target plural or target segmentation label in fitting. Test 229-wpm held recordings of both forms; primary score covers only the opposite, previously unheard form per installation (16 distinct recordings per voice). Require at least 13/16 joint kind/count and ≥2 extra correct over both free and bare-repeat controls per voice. Check the rotated-name and whole-lesson exemplar controls, old-word retention at 223 wpm and target singular consistency. Disclose when controls leave little improvement possible. Physical utterance duration and number of examples reported separately.

Post-primary retention diagnosis: the reader used original marked recordings only for suffix learning while saving new marked recordings as stem exemplars. Preserve the prefixes from the very same original source cuts as additional templates for the old kinds, at zero extra label cost. Cross this symmetric storage with each frozen enrollment arm. Confirm on 241-wpm re-recordings of all 16 kinds in both inflections without changing any cut or other parameter. Require symmetric marked enrollment to reduce old correct-to-wrong flips vs its own no-extra baseline by at least half relative to asymmetric enrollment at that rate, and match or exceed symmetric bare-repeat on novel plurals; report gains and eligibility per voice, including the mixture of old vs new decisions. This is separate from the original primary test.

At 229 wpm, joint unseen-plural accuracy (no extra / repeat / marked / whole / rotated) was 13/13/15/14/8 out of 16 Samantha and 7/10/10/9/2 Joelle. Both learned 16/16 practiced singulars across installations. Only Samantha met the primary criterion; paired eight-stem bootstrap intervals for marked minus repeat were [0,5] and [−3,3] extra outputs. Every missed marked plural retained the correct number. For example Samantha recovered plural tavi and navi but confused gavi with tavi. Original 223-wpm guard judgments fell 20→14/32 and 28→18/32 under marked enrollment (16 distinct old-word recordings per voice shared across two installations). Eight marked lessons lasted 5.60–5.86 s versus 4.76–4.78 s for eight bare repeats.

At 241 wpm asymmetric marked enrollment again displaced six Samantha and eleven Joelle previously correct old judgments. Adding the original stem exemplars raised marked retention from 12/32 and 19/32 to 32/32 each with zero new old-word losses. In that expanded bank marked enrollment gave 6/16 and 8/16 novel plurals versus bare-repeat 4/16 and 3/16. However the asymmetric marked bank got 13/16 on BOTH voices: keeping the old exemplars caused substantial interference in the other direction. All 18 fresh errors across the two symmetric marked plural panels selected an old kind with the correct number. At equal value per decision and the measured conditional accuracies, symmetric storage beats asymmetric marked storage when the old share of an old/novel-plural mixture exceeds 7/17 on Samantha or 10/23 on Joelle; no workload prevalence or deployment break-even established. Both retention criteria passed, but one marked singular has not solved joint old/new recognition. Every marked-arm decision checked against registered speech-score-connected on the Mac (all 64 confirmation decisions per voice and storage arm). Raw WAVs, guessed cut frames, predictions and verifier in external morph_enroll.py VOICE prepare/score, morph_enroll_check.py VOICE, morph_retain.py VOICE, morph_retain_check.py VOICE and morph_retain_summary.py through mac.sh.

### Predicting the other inflection from familiar pronunciation changes

Freeze before scoring: reuse the 16 kinds, installments and source recordings from the retention study. Compare four kind readers conditional on the SAME symmetric segment reader's inferred suffix: that segment reader, whole-word nearest template copying the offered new singular as its candidate for the other suffix, matching the first half of the same whole-word templates, and transferring the mean paired change in normalized whole-word spectral envelopes from the eight original stems. Familiar kinds always keep their real form-specific templates. Learn only the mean difference, with no target-plural feedback; reversing singular/other reverses the direction. Compare sign-reversed transfer and an evaluator-only known-count arm. Original 241-wpm rate diagnostic; confirm separately at 253 wpm. Primary per voice ≥13/16 unheard plurals, no more than one lost old judgment against the prior symmetric bank, and ≥2 more plurals correct than the better of copy and first-half controls. Verify its acoustic prediction error on the actually recorded unseen forms in addition to classification, reporting old/novel competition and crossed directions. No change to source training or search after opening outcomes.

At the fresh 253-wpm rate, segment / copy / first-half / transfer / reversed transfer yielded 6/0/0/1/0 of 16 novel plurals on Samantha and 7/1/0/9/0 on Joelle. Every arm retained all 32 old judgments (16 recordings under two installations) and all 16 taught-form judgments per voice. The 13/16 criterion failed on both. On Joelle transfer beat copy by eight, eight-stem paired bootstrap 95% interval [3,13]; on Samantha it beat both copying controls by one [0,3] while losing five vs segmentation. At the opened 241-wpm rate the transferred reader also got 1/16 and 9/16. At both rates every novel count was heard correctly. All 16 novel templates per voice at each rate were closer to the actual other form after adding the learned mean change than after copying. At 253 wpm mean squared error by direction went 1.795→1.027 and 1.792→1.022 Samantha, 1.526→.862 and 1.516→.827 Joelle. Improved reconstruction did not ensure the correct name ranked ahead of competitors.

Evaluator-only exclusion of all familiar kinds raised transfer to 11/16 Samantha and 10/16 Joelle. Competition from actual familiar recordings accounts for ten Samantha failures but only one Joelle failure that this intervention could repair. This is a diagnostic with privileged membership; no live policy receives that bit. Rotating identities in one half of the source pairs changes the estimated shift by at most 9e−16 because subtracting the two group means is sufficient. The result supports a direction-specific average acoustic change; it supplies no evidence of learning individual stem correspondences. Separate verifier rebuilt every predicted template as an average of eight independently transported exemplars, confirmed the chosen name and template errors, and reproduced archived segment outputs at 241. External morph_predict.py VOICE renders/scores, morph_predict_check.py VOICE verifies, and morph_predict_competition.py performs the diagnostic under mac.sh. All raw audio and results saved locally.

### Calibrating comparisons between heard and predicted inflections

Freeze a source-only lesson in how to score the preceding predicted pronunciations. Rotate all 70 splits of the eight old kinds into four heard in both inflections/four heard only in the singular. Use 185-wpm templates and 223-wpm old-kind practice. In each rotation estimate the mean whole-envelope ending change ONLY from the four fully exposed stems. Learn a single allowance subtracted from the squared distance to a predicted opposite form; no adjustment to an observed template. Fit each voice using both suffix directions and equal weighting of old vs simulated novel plurals, maximizing the worse group accuracy, then their average, then smallest absolute allowance. Search all source decision crossings, intermediate values, zero and both exterior intervals. Freeze before opening independent 235-wpm target recordings; reuse all previous real exposure rates and the registered count reader. Compare no allowance, forcing ALL candidates to use the previous ending plus its mean change, original segment reader, and source-calibrated allowance. Do not provide the tested kind or membership. Require ≥2 additional novel judgments over no allowance, at most one newly lost old judgment and ≥13/16 novel words per voice. Report a target-only threshold ceiling under the same old-word guard to separate poor transfer of the allowance from wrong ranking inside either candidate group. Report established names and re-heard singulars separately.

Old-kind practice chose allowances .6980 Samantha and .5322 Joelle in normalized squared spectral distance. On the rotated source decisions, accuracy on pseudo-novel plurals rose from 140 to 367/560 and 315 to 395/560, retaining 558/560 previously correct fully observed judgments per voice; those rotations share original words and recordings. On the fresh 235-wpm targets, raw / calibrated / all-cross-ending / segment joint novel accuracy was 1/11/7/6 of 16 Samantha and 8/10/9/7 of 16 Joelle. Old judgments 32/31/25/32 and 32/32/27/32, with 16/16 taught-form judgments maintained in every arm. Thus the extra-correct and retention margins passed on both voices; the 13/16 absolute criterion failed both. Paired eight-stem bootstrap intervals for additional correct over raw were [6,14] and [0,5]. Single control offset was selected entirely before fresh rendering; 32 unique test recordings per voice (each replayed in two teaching conventions).

Exhaustive evaluator sweep over all target decision crossings with ≤1 old loss yielded ceilings 11/16 and 10/16. Supplying the correct candidate group also gave exactly 11 and 10: changing the allowance alone cannot recover another plural on either voice with these candidates. All eleven remaining wrong judgments ranked an incorrect novel template above the intended one, even where a familiar template won overall. Both voices misidentified gavi with either ending; both chose biko for gavika despite hearing the correct count. No degradation of count recognition. Independent audit reconstructed score decisions, checked distinct confirmation WAVs, and compared within-stem matched gains. Reproduce with external morph_allow.py VOICE fit for both voices before morph_allow.py VOICE test, then morph_allow_check.py VOICE under mac.sh. Source rotation distances, frozen allowances and target candidate group margins are saved alongside the raw audio.

### Recognizing a stem by its acoustic onset across inflections

Freeze the previous word-group decision, suffix reader and allowance. Only when the reader already selects the partially taught group and predicts its unheard ending may it change which member it selects. Compare the new waveform against each candidate's taught marked singular from speech onset. Use 8, 16 or 24 source frames (10-ms hop), choosing the length and either fixed timing or dynamic alignment on old words at 185→223 wpm only. Dynamic alignment starts at both onsets, moves (1,1), (1,2) or (2,1) and allows target endpoints at .67–1.5 times the source prefix duration. Score by accumulated mean squared spectral distance weighted by source frames consumed, divided by prefix length. Equal weight to both ending directions. For each voice select the best within each timing family on exact eight-way opposite-ending kind recognition; ties prefer shorter prefixes. Primary dynamic reader must get ≥13/16 novel plurals and ≥2 over the frozen previous reader per voice, with zero extra old or practiced-singular losses. Compare the best source-selected fixed timing, and separate whether warping itself helps. Two fresh 217-wpm voice panels, same stems. Oracle novel membership and fixed group mistakes are distinct diagnoses; do not reroute on evaluation labels.

On 16 old-word practice contrasts, the best dynamic lengths were 8 frames Samantha (13/16, tied 24 frames) and 24 Joelle (14/16); fixed timing selected 8 for both (11/16 and 12/16). On the fresh 217-wpm unfamiliar plurals, calibrated full template / selected fixed onset / selected dynamic onset scored 11/9/9 Samantha and 11/12/11 Joelle out of 16. All arms retained 32/32 old judgments and 16/16 practiced-form judgments per voice. No primary transfer gain. Replacing the dynamic path by ordinary fixed matching at the same chosen window also achieved 9 and 12. Eight-name paired bootstrap intervals for dynamic minus calibrated were [−6,0] and [−3,3] additional correct. The group gate routed 16/16 and 15/16 novel items to the onset comparison. Even granting membership, the dynamic reader got only 9 and 11.

Samantha's short window confused navi with nelo in both directions of inflection. Post hoc frozen candidate sweep on these same recordings: for fixed windows 8/16/24 the scores were 9/13/13 Samantha and 12/11/12 Joelle; dynamic 9/12/13 and 10/11/11. Longer context explains some Samantha headroom, while selecting any one tested setting with hindsight still tops out at 12/16 Joelle. The previous calibrated reader alone was correct on three Samantha/one Joelle items missed by the selected dynamic reader; dynamic alone on one per voice, both wrong on four each. An item-specific oracle over all six windows plus baseline reached 14/16 and 13/16, with no demonstrated mechanism to select them. A recursive path enumerator independently reproduced all 512 candidate distances per voice at the selected dynamic setting. Each voice contributed 32 confirmation recordings; acoustic envelopes came from the registered local service, alignment and reranking ran in external replay. No listener change was promoted. External morph_onset.py VOICE fit/run, morph_onset_check.py VOICE and morph_onset_diagnose.py under mac.sh reproduce source choices, raw judgments and diagnostics.

### Asking for the bare form while retaining the heard number

Freeze a single paid clarification operation: the speaker returns the intended kind's isolated bare form; only that recording, never the referent handle, enters the reply reader. Keep the calibrated whole-form listener and its original number decision. On familiar kinds only, use the 70 half-enrollment rotations, 185-wpm references and 223-wpm simulated requests; include both already seen and opposite endings. Train a threshold on the difference between the best and second-best adjusted kind costs, to ask when the margin is small. Score both skip and actual decoded 201-wpm bare replies against eight registered 173-wpm templates; upper bound of one call, price .15 correct outputs. Fit maximum source net with fewest calls on ties. Freeze before new 227-wpm requests and 193-wpm replies (16 possible names). Compare never, always, equal-spend random in expectation, withheld paid reply, alternative valid word after payment, and hindsight repair ceiling. Primary per voice: ≥13/16 novel correct, ≥1 net unit over strongest fixed action across 64 decisions, and positive advantage over matched random. Record numbers recovered vs spoiled and individual audio time. The teacher operation and word boundaries are supplied; the price and judgments are simulated.

Frozen margin thresholds .20206 Samantha and .20914 Joelle selected 411/2240 and 354/2240 old-word source rotation decisions. The 16 bare replies per test voice all decoded correctly against the original 16-name bank. On 64 target decisions per voice, novel-plural accuracy rose 11→16/16 Samantha and 10→16/16 Joelle, preserving 32/32 familiar and 16/16 taught-form judgments. Margin policy asked 24 times to repair five errors (14.94 s of reply audio) and 13 times to repair six (7.92 s); zero correct decisions spoiled. In Samantha's case all 16 unheard plurals triggered requests; in Joelle's only eight did. At .15 per request, learned / skip / always / matched random net was 60.40/59/54.40/57.275 and 62.05/58/54.40/57.269. All preregistered point targets passed. Stem-paired bootstrap intervals for net vs best fixed action [−2.20,5.55] and [.65,6.90]; the first voice's advantage remains uncertain. A post hoc cheap conditional control asking whenever the inferred kind belonged to the partially enrolled group with the opposite suffix earned 61.60 and 60.75, beating the learned policy on Samantha. Hindsight ceilings 63.25 and 63.10. Withhold the paid reply: net 55.40/56.05; alternative-word audio instead: 36.40/49.05. Supplied operations and target distributions limit these economics; they exclude historical label/compute costs and synthesis. These 64 decisions share 32 inflected recordings and 16 bare replies per voice. The threshold models when to invoke the supplied clarification; no spoken question was generated.

Exact integer objective independently verified both source threshold choices. Bare replies and original suffix decisions ran through the registered local acoustic services. External morph_clarify.py VOICE fit then run, morph_clarify_check.py VOICE and morph_clarify_replay.py under mac.sh reproduce measurements and examples. Samantha's example plays gavimo, initially heard as the other ending-marked name tavimo, followed by the bare gavi that restores the correct name while preserving number. Joelle's example repairs navi initially mistaken for nelo. Raw recordings and per-decision calls, receipts and alternatives saved locally.

### Retaining a word learned through clarification

Freeze the 227-wpm episode's paid queries separately per voice and singular convention. After that batch, store the normalized original request sound labeled only by the decoded bare reply and the original heard ending. Persist numerical exemplars outside the repo; use a fresh process for testing. For a label/ending with a newly observed form, replace its inferred form and associated allowance by that observed exemplar; for previously observed forms retain both and use the closer. Keep every other base score and the clarification threshold fixed. No new target labels for model selection. Compare true storage, erased, alternate valid receipts, and writing only where a paid reply contradicts the initial hearing. Test an entire subsequent batch at fresh 249 wpm, with memory frozen during the batch. Measure first-hearing gains on the previous novel-plural panel separately from later paid repair. Require ≥3 extra correct before asking, no previously correct familiar/taught-form losses, and ≥25% fewer calls than erasure without lower final net, per voice. Charge .15 per question across both batches and .02 per stored exemplar once; matched receipt interventions have identical initial query charges. Audit paid vs unqueried forms separately. This is word-form learning across renditions, with no expectation of zero-shot transfer to unseen stems.

On fresh 249-wpm renditions, storing all verified replies raised unaided recognition of the originally unseen plurals from 11→16/16 Samantha and 10→16/16 Joelle. Paired eight-stem 95% bootstrap intervals for gains [1,9] and [2,11]. All previously correct familiar and taught-form judgments survived. Across 64 second-batch decisions per voice, stored/erased first-hearing correctness 64/58 and 63/57; both arms eventually achieved 64/64 after any clarification. Samantha calls 23→0, Joelle 16→9. The remaining Joelle mistake was an old mepo form also missed after erasure. In Joelle's eight unqueried novel forms the erased and retained banks both achieved 8/8, whereas on the eight queried forms they achieved 2/8 and 8/8. All 16 Samantha novel forms had been queried, so no unqueried comparison there. All count judgments stayed correct. All preregistered criteria passed on both voices.

Full / changed-only / erased two-batch modeled net (128 decisions including the identical paid first batch) 123.92/121.45/120.95 Samantha and 124.44/124.13/123.65 Joelle. Writing only contradicted first hearings stored five/six examples, then triggered 19/12 calls. Writing every reply stored 24/13 examples across the two isolated conventions, consuming 61,276/33,644 bytes of local NPZ artifacts including headers. Paid confirmations of already correct guesses can therefore reduce repeat requests. Replacing only the stored reply by another valid decoded name reduced novel first-hearing scores to 0/16 and 3/16, two-batch net 106.72 and 116.69. This intervention leaves first-batch conversation unchanged and changes what gets retained. Same synthetic voices, lexical items and known suffix conventions in both batches; scores demonstrate durable recognition of clarified forms across speed, without testing new speakers or transfer to new names.

Separate fresh processes reloaded the acoustic exemplars. Independent candidate-by-candidate replay reproduced the second batch, and source checks reconstructed each stored waveform feature and its label from its own paid reply. All 32 second-batch waveforms per voice differed bytewise from first-batch recordings. Use external morph_remember.py VOICE save/test and morph_remember_check.py VOICE through mac.sh; serialized memory, raw decisions and morph-retained-clarification.wav saved locally. The replay contains Samantha's initial gavimo, bare-name reply gavi, then the fresh gavimo recognized without another question.

### Can a clarification from another speaker improve first hearing?

Freeze the two installed voices and their true 227-wpm receipt-labeled memory files. In both directions the receiver already knows local bare names, eight old marked pairs at 185 wpm, and one marked example per new name at 201 wpm. It receives no clarification from its own 227-wpm run in the primary arm. Align source to target channels by centered ridge regression over normalized-time frames of the 16 matched old marked recordings (regularizer .01 times mean diagonal covariance). For a stored word/ending predict its local prototype, then add the mapped difference between the source recording and the source's predicted prototype. This residual rule conditions on the local pronunciation already available. Treat the result as an observed exemplar for the previous scoring rule. Compare erased, raw imported, affine absolute import, rotating the residual labels among novel names, actual independently acquired local memory, and a target-voice oracle bank matched to the imported labels/coverage. Verify at a new 231-wpm rendition; fit maps before rendering. Require per direction ≥2 additional correct novel plurals over erasure, no additional familiar/taught-form errors, and ≥25% fewer requests at no lower final net. Report covered and uncovered words, reply reliance, map exposure and actual source-memory cost. A common label space and paired source/target examples are supplied. This tests transfer between two known voice banks; no unexposed-speaker claim.

Cross-voice correction failed. Receiving Joelle (Samantha donor): unheard plural first-hearing erased/local/raw/absolute/residual/rotated/matched local oracle = 10/16/0/0/1/0/16 out of 16; all 16 forms covered by Samantha's paid receipts. Receiving Samantha (Joelle donor): 11/16/7/7/7/5/15. Of the eight covered Samantha forms erased→imported went 5→0; the other eight went 6→7 as competitors changed. Residual minus erased paired eight-stem bootstrap intervals [−13,−5] and [−8,0] respectively. Both preserved 32/32 familiar and 16/16 taught-form judgments, but neither met transfer accuracy or 25% call reduction. Test calls erased→residual: Joelle 13→16, final correct 64→63/64, target net 62.05→60.60; Samantha 23→18, final correct 64→64, net 60.55→61.30. Fewer questions in the latter direction accompanied worse unaided hearing. Independently purchased local memory yielded 16/16 on both voices. Donor cost was 24 or 13 prior requests (.15 each) and saved examples (.02 each); the map additionally used the same 16 old inflected utterances in both voices with supplied correspondence. These acquisition costs are excluded from target net.

Across all covered plural forms, imported residuals improved the target envelope prediction on 0/16 and 0/8 recordings. Mean squared error rose .851→1.283 for Joelle and 1.102→1.418 for Samantha. A post hoc scoring intervention restored the allowance for *predicted* forms while still replacing the original template: 8/16 for both. Keeping the local prediction in competition with the import instead gave exactly the erased accuracy in both directions, with or without the allowance. The local oracle on matched labels isolates representation and scoring from which examples happened to be purchased. This does not establish that the labels or semantics fail to travel, only that these voice-transformed exemplars failed to improve an already enrolled listener. The receivers used the same supervised word identifiers and supplied conventions. Independent augmented least-squares fit verified the ridge map and every arm's decisions were reconstructed per label. External morph_crossmem.py SOURCE TARGET fit/run, morph_crossmem_check.py SOURCE TARGET and morph_crossmem_weight.py under mac.sh reproduce the scores, maps and matched interventions. Raw data and transformed-score diagnostics saved locally. No cross-voice import was promoted.

### Transfer of spoken number agreement with two competing nouns

Freeze a bounded construction: two separately presented inflected nouns then a spoken predicate whose form agrees with either the first or the second noun. Both controller conventions use exactly the same noun audio. Fit donor-side predictors from three count configurations (00,01,10), four old noun pairs each; reserve 11 and all target noun stems. Prefer the smallest subset of heard count positions whose table reproduces the predicate's heard count; ties unresolved, never infer an unseen table cell. Compare a full two-position table, first-only, last-only, and no transfer. Receiver has local acoustic templates and two grounded number examples per word class, but zero target sentences. Reverse the noun ending-to-count mapping, independently preserve or reverse the predicate's mapping, and give each voice its own two predicate pronunciations. Export only the fitted dependency and count table. Test both communities and all four markings on eight new ordered target noun pairs per direction (cyclic adjacent names, seeded shuffle 172101); cross predicate mappings and require complete correctness ≥7/8 pair sets per direction, ≥20 points over always choosing the closest noun on the two conflict patterns over matched controller worlds. Score the unseen 11 configuration independently. Withhold the donor's conflict evidence, switch the donor controller, and introduce a hidden target controller reversal only as an impossibility control. Word boundaries, noun/predicate roles, semantic count anchors, candidate output buttons and subset/table bias are supplied. No target test labels in fitting. Report structural and acoustic denominators separately.

Both donor fits selected the correct single noun position after 12 teaching sequences per controller convention. On transfer each receiver passed 8/8 complete noun-pair sets, 128/128 individual decisions, 64/64 conflicting-number cases, and 32/32 withheld double-plural cases. Fixed first or fixed last each scored 32/64 on conflict; switching only the transferred controller scored 0/64 on the identical conflict audio, preserving every common-number answer. The full two-position table matched the 96 seen-configuration decisions but abstained on all 32 double-plurals. With only 00 demonstrations the minimal fit became constant singular, correct on 64/128 and no complete pair sets. Erasure deferred throughout. Hidden target controller reversal with no distinguishing target teaching has the same 0/64 mixed-case result as the switched-donor intervention. Both preregistered thresholds passed. The local hearings recovered all tested noun counts. Only 16 physical target noun recordings and two 233-wpm predicate buttons per voice were reused across these forks; source sequences reuse 16 old noun recordings and two predicate forms, and there are only two synthetic voices and two possible target conventions here.

No source pronunciation exemplars entered the receiver. It received a positional rule and a small semantic table; target-local number grounding still used labeled isolated examples for nouns and predicates, an existing enrolled noun reader, and known turn boundaries. The two counts and position-subset inductive bias are supplied. This demonstrates conditional agreement-rule transfer in this miniature fixed construction. It does not establish acquisition of noun phrases, discovery of word boundaries, free speech production, or evidence that another community must share the donor's convention. The paired replay repeats the same two presegmented Samantha nouns (nelo singleton, biko plural); the imported convention alone selects darra then darrin. Independent enumeration verified target answers and matched inputs across controller forks. External number_agree.py VOICE builds rules; number_agree.py SOURCE TARGET loads them in another process; number_agree_check.py verifies and makes number-agree-samantha-pair.wav under mac.sh. Source-only rule files, inputs and decisions saved locally.

### Number agreement from a single connected recording

Reuse the frozen donor agreement rules, reversed target noun markings and exact eight held noun pairs. Render both nouns together in one 231-wpm TTS utterance, no inserted silence or boundary given to the reader. Retain a supplied four-part stem/ending/stem/ending pattern, all 16 stems and both endings legal at both positions. Primary reader uses registered joint acoustic decoding and eight extra connected target-voice examples containing only old stems at 193 wpm. Teach ordered stem/ending identities without times; supply no target agreement/predicate demonstrations. Compare zero connected examples, fixed midpoint split, and archived independent isolated recordings. Freeze old pairs (i, i+3 mod 8), ending bits (i mod 2, floor(i/2) mod 2). Require ≥7/8 complete sets across four count patterns, both donor controllers and both predicate mappings, and ≥90% on conflicting-number decisions in each transfer direction. Report first vs second ending errors, effect of lessons, and full stem readings separately. One recorded noun pair creates four semantic forks; report unique-audio denominators. No inference of word count, role discovery or unsupervised segmentation claim.

On single uninterrupted TTS noun-pair recordings, the primary trained joint decoder passed only 3/8 complete sets per receiver, versus 8/8 from isolated renditions. Samantha: 110/128 predicate choices, 54/64 conflict; Joelle: 114/128, 58/64 conflict. Only Joelle met the 90% mixed-case threshold; both missed the complete-set threshold. The first noun ending was correct on 23/32 Samantha and 25/32 Joelle; the second was correct on 32/32 for both. Transferred rules and predicate selection alone had zero errors conditional on both endings being correct. Thus all incorrect predicate choices were caused by a misheard first ending when the convention required that noun. The joint decoder recovered entire stem/ending sequences on only 1/32 Samantha and 0/32 Joelle. Agreement success is substantially easier than identifying the nouns in this task.

Without connected teaching, unconstrained joint search scored 4/8 and 3/8 complete sets (116/128 and 108/128). The extra eight supervised recordings hurt Samantha's ending recognition and helped Joelle's. Splitting at the midpoint with source-only templates gave 3/8 and 0/8. In a separate matched-bank diagnostic imposing the nearest legal midpoint for the noun boundary while retaining all four positional acoustic costs gave 5/8 and 6/8; both had first/second endings 28/32 and 32/32, and 120/128 predicate choices. That midpoint is a heuristic, not an observed phonetic boundary. Example: in Samantha's “bikoka rulomo” both endings were read as mo; the resulting predicate was wrong under first-noun agreement and correct under last-noun agreement. The 32 held recordings per voice each have four counterfactual evaluations with identical input. Both donor rule files and isolated controls were reused unchanged. A separate enumerator searched all three cut positions and reproduced every primary four-handle reading for both voices. External agree_connected.py SOURCE TARGET and agree_connected_check.py SOURCE TARGET under mac.sh reproduce the source lessons, WAVs, all arm decisions and the boundary constraint diagnostic. No general-purpose connected language claim from this panel.

### Locating an internal number ending under uneven timing

Compare a cut at a fraction of elapsed time with a cut at a fraction of cumulative spectral movement (Euclidean frame-to-frame distance after the existing channel scaling). Neither sees a true word boundary. Fit each fraction from {.35,.40,.45,.50,.55,.60,.65}, by most correct first-and-second endings jointly on eight old 193-wpm lessons under leave-one-recording-out acoustic enrollment. Train on identity and power-law time warps .8/1.25; ties prefer nearest .5, then smaller. Freeze before rendering 219-wpm fresh old-test-stem pairings, cyclic offset three instead of one, seed/order unchanged; exclude all exact teaching and earlier test pairs. Score the same 32 acoustic recordings per voice under identity and stronger opposing warps (2/3,1.5) applied after speech-envelope extraction. The warp interpolates across the entire utterance with no boundary input. Compare travel, fitted elapsed, fixed midpoint, unrestricted acoustic segmentation and evaluator-only mapped clean midpoint; the last is a timing diagnostic, not a phonetic annotation. Primary: ≥3 extra first endings over both elapsed baselines on each distorted panel per voice, ≥28/32 correct first endings there, and ≤1 clean first-ending loss vs fixed midpoint. Report second endings, paired-warp agreement and per-boundary choices. Preserve old source bank and agreement rules.

Leave-one-old-recording-out selection chose elapsed/travel fractions .50/.40 Samantha and .55/.60 Joelle. On fresh pairs, first ending correct (unwarped / exponent 2/3 / exponent 1.5, each /32): Samantha travel 25/27/23, fitted time and fixed midpoint both 27/17/21, free joint search 26/23/25. Joelle travel 26/21/26, fitted time 23/20/25, fixed midpoint 30/22/21, free search 24/20/23. Every method preserved 32/32 second endings in every condition. Spectral movement shifted the mean guessed cut in the correct temporal direction: .279→.513 of elapsed duration across distortions Samantha and .449→.651 Joelle. Across both distortions of each recording, joint success was 20/32 vs 12/32 fitted elapsed for Samantha, 17/32 vs 17/32 Joelle. Paired eight-noun-pair bootstrap intervals on additional first endings over fitted time: Samantha [4,15] and [−2,7], Joelle [−3,5] and [−9,12]. Neither voice met the full robustness or clean-retention criteria. No improvement promoted.

Transporting the clean midpoint using the known evaluator warp gave first-ending 29/30 across the two distorted Samantha panels and 29/26 for Joelle (counts each out of 32). Every one of the 32 recordings per voice under each transformation admitted at least one legal cut recovering both true endings; mean fraction of legal cuts doing so ranged .568–.642 on distorted panels. This latter sweep uses intended endings and is a feasibility ceiling, not a measurable true word boundary. Source counts are eight utterances/voice with rotations sharing recordings; the test consists of 32 independently synthesized noun strings/voice plus feature-space transformations, not natural changes of speaker tempo. All free-search readings verified against registered speech-score-connected; a separate implementation reconstructed the time/travel/mapped cuts and checked held-pair disjointness. External agree_travel.py VOICE fit/test, agree_travel_check.py VOICE and agree_travel_ceiling.py VOICE under mac.sh reproduce the stored fractions, raw cut choices and diagnostics.

### Using a later predicate to revise a partly heard noun

Keep the two frozen agreement rules and local grounded count meanings. Score both possible first endings from the uninterrupted pair by registered constrained searches, with either second ending; repeat for the second slot. Calibrate one acoustic temperature on the eight old lessons using leave-one-recording-out banks, identity/.8/1.25 feature warps and log loss over both positions; temperature grid .01,.03,.1,.3,1,3. Treat the resulting two acoustic posteriors as independent. Reweight the four possible count pairs according to compatibility with the heard predicate under the transferred rule. Infer predicate fidelity from observed settled histories: 15/16 correct or 8/16 correct with Laplace smoothing. For held trials evaluate both truthful and alternative predicate audio, weighting by those respective population fidelities, without a truth flag at decision time. Target: new directed pairings (cyclic +5) at 211 wpm, same eight new names, two explicit turns (continuous noun pair then isolated predicate), retained test warp exponents 2/3 and 1.5. Both controller worlds see identical noun audio. Primary in each voice: ≥5-point first-number accuracy gain over noun-only when the first noun controls the reliable predicate, no change in first-number choice when second controls or predicate is chance, and lower first-number Brier loss than history-conditioned predicate-copy on reliable first-control trials. Also score masking and inverting the paid-free predicate at identical noun audio and report whether a simpler copy rule wins on accuracy. Test grammatical use of subsequent evidence, not improved segmentation.

At reliable first-noun agreement, expected first-number accuracy rose 79.69→90.72% Samantha and 70.31→92.68% Joelle. These are truth/alternative-predicate weighted scores on 64 hearings per voice (32 independent strings × two feature warps, eight noun pairs). Gains +11.04/+22.36 points; pair-bootstrap 95% intervals [−1.17,26.56] and [7.23,37.70] points. Masking the predicate restored the initial posterior. Swapping only predicate audio changed 31/64 and 59/64 first-number choices respectively in the reliable first-controller condition; it changed none under second-noun control or chance fidelity. Example: on Samantha's warped neloka saromo, estimated probability of a plural first noun went .355→.815 after the appropriate predicate, with the noun evidence fixed. Reversing both teacher-predicate alternatives at the same weights reduced expected accuracy to 48.34% and 12.01%.

Simply following the relevant reliable predicate achieved 93.75% expected accuracy in both voices, outperforming fusion. Brier losses noun-only / fusion / relevant predicate-copy .1717/.0721/.0610 Samantha and .1959/.0572/.0610 Joelle. Thus both passed the improvement and selectivity tests; only Joelle passed probability calibration against copy. Conditional on the predicate actually being false, fusion retained 45.31% correct on Samantha and 6.25% on Joelle; copy missed all. Acoustic temperatures .1/.3 fitted only on eight old recordings per voice. The probabilistic update, conditional independence assumption, supplied reliability counts and both counterfactual predicate forms are explicit fixtures. The zero effect of irrelevant predicates follows from factorization and the learned controller, not from discovering statistical independence in this evaluation. Number meanings, word classes, two-turn boundary and local predicate exemplars were already grounded. No decoding boundary within the two-noun audio was supplied. Independent checks recovered every posterior via binary odds, along with the masked/inverted controls. Run external agree_revise.py VOICE (source acoustic fit) then agree_revise.py SOURCE TARGET and agree_revise_check.py TARGET under mac.sh. All decision-time costs, predicate probabilities and recordings saved locally.

### Learning spoken cues to the reliability of an agreement report

Add two anonymous isolated cue forms to the existing noun-pair/predicate exchange. Cross two speaker habits: either cue may accompany the reliable (15/16) or chance (1/2) predicate. With the same noun and predicate waveforms, only the cue and earlier settled experience can distinguish their evidential value. At each of 32 independent history draws per habit supply 16 verified predicate/scene outcomes per cue (also score 0 and 4 per cue); couple draws across reversed habits by exchanging cue identities. No reliability label or true probability in fitting. Learn separate Laplace-smoothed frequencies from decoded cue and predicate and publicly settled count, versus one pooled frequency, never trusting the predicate, and a hard switch choosing between noun and predicate based on which cue has higher empirical accuracy. Preserve the previous acoustic temperature and imported agreement rule. Primary: on first-controller trials per voice, at 16 examples/cue reduce Brier loss by ≥.01 vs pooling and increase expected accuracy by ≥2 points; separately require cue exchange to reverse the decision in the appropriate direction under BOTH habits on ≥25% of matched noun/predicate conflicts (exclude exact noun ties). Confirm zero cue effect when second noun controls; measure calibration with actually equal-reliability cues (both 23/32). Use fresh pairs cyclic offset seven, 215 wpm, warps 2/3 and 1.5. Evaluate truthful/alternative predicates weighted by the fixture probabilities, no access to truth during revision. Seed 172301; histories are simulated feedback about grounded meanings, not new human speakers. Supplied cue boundaries, multiplicative evidence model and permission to index counts by cue. No claim that the sounds have general natural-language evidential meanings.

After 16 settled examples per cue in the contrasting-fidelity population, cue-specific vs pooled expected first-number accuracy 80.89/76.88% Samantha and 84.04/80.19% Joelle, Brier .12970/.16328 and .11258/.14297. After four per cue gains were 3.03 and 3.29 points; with zero, all cue effects were exactly zero. At 16, exchanging only the cue under BOTH opposed habits switched the decision in the warranted direction on 46.48%/92.53% of conflict pairs (64 noun hearings, one contrary predicate per hearing, 32 paired history draws; 2048 paired opportunities per voice). Independent four-world enumeration recovered the posteriors and verified no effect on the first number when the predicate agreed with the second. Both voices met all primary point thresholds. History-draw-only bootstrap 95% intervals for accuracy gain [3.38,4.65] and [3.28,4.42] points; these do not express uncertainty over new voices or phrases.

Strong simpler control, copy the predicate on the historically more reliable cue and otherwise keep the noun: 82.03%/85.16% correct, beating soft weighting on accuracy in both voices. In the equal-fidelity population pooling was better: 77.11 vs 76.37% Samantha and 79.49 vs 78.83% Joelle, with Brier .16249 vs .16465 and .14521 vs .14878. The cue-specific learner interpreted some accidental differences as meaningful. This is a learned speaker-local association with report reliability in a bounded task. It used an assigned isolated cue position, two familiar synthesized sounds, a supplied agreement/evidence model, simulated settled outcomes, and no feedback from test phrases. There were 32 distinct new connected noun recordings and two feature-space rate transformations per voice, two isolated predicate forms and two cue forms reused across histories and matched forks; no natural-language hedge or sentence-boundary discovery claim. External agree_cue.py SOURCE TARGET and agree_cue_check.py SOURCE TARGET under mac.sh reproduce raw acoustic evidence, history outcomes, cue-switched probabilities and summaries. All artifacts saved locally.

### Inferring whether two spoken cues warrant separate reliability estimates

Compare one shared reliability vs two independent reliabilities, each free Bernoulli rate having a uniform Beta prior. Give models equal prior mass. Update posterior model weights from the ordered public outcomes only; average their predictive rates before combining with noun evidence. No test regime labels in any policy. Strong fixed control: choose one constant separate-model weight from 0,.1,…,1 minimizing balanced Brier loss on the other voice's archived 215-wpm speech plus its original 16-per-cue histories (tie favors pooling). Also retain pure split, pure pool, hard model selection and the earlier hard cue/predicate switch. Freeze weight before scoring an independent set of 256 paired history draws (seed 172401) on the archived 211-wpm noun panel, held out from constant-weight fitting. Test two equally weighted new regimes (.90/.50 and .70/.70), with an intermediate .80/.60 scored separately. Cross the assignment of sounds to reliability. Check prefixes of 0,4,16,64 verified outcomes per cue. Primary at 16 per cue: gain ≥.002 Brier over strongest fixed mixture on balanced regimes per voice, excess Brier ≤.002 vs pure pool when cues are identical, and ≥.01 improvement vs pure pool under contrast. Report accuracies, learned distinction weight, paired uncertainty and 64-example behavior even if 16-example test misses. Include same-count cue-label permutation and histories with identical totals but different cue-specific counts. This tests a supplied model-comparison mechanism in local replay.

At 16 observations per cue the inferred probability that the cues needed separate rates averaged .793 in the contrasting population, .373 with equal rates and .511 with the intermediate contrast. Identical totals of 22 correct reports out of 32 supported distinct interpretations: counts (16,6) gave .999 probability of separate rates, (11,11) gave .279. Contrast Brier improved from .15417 to .13556 Samantha and .17253 to .14861 Joelle versus pooling; expected accuracy rose 78.28→81.62% and 74.48→78.23%. Permuting the cue labels in training returned contrast accuracy to 78.29% and 74.39%. Under equal reliability, excess Brier versus pooling was .00063/.00132, within the .002 tolerance. The hard switch still won on contrast accuracy (84.67%/79.96%).

The primary balanced gain vs the opposite-voice development-selected constant weight (.8/1.0 respectively) was +.00066 [+.00045,+.00085] and −.00046 [−.00078,−.00013], missing the prespecified +.002 on both voices. At four examples the balanced gains were negative on both; at 64 they reached +.00217 and +.00039. At 64 the mean separate-rate weight reached .993 for contrast and .264 for equal rates. The intermediate regime at 16 still favored the fixed mixture on Brier for both voices. Intervals are paired bootstraps over 256 simulated histories with the archived noun hearings fixed. The sounds, two fixed candidate models and prior were supplied. Each voice had 32 connected noun recordings replayed at two feature warps. Registered acoustic cue recognition ran locally; noun evidence and predicate readings were cached. Reversing cue assignments was checked by exact symmetry, not counted as another sample. Independently verified all model weights by sequential prediction and all model scores under both cue assignments. External cue_pool.py SOURCE TARGET fits the weight, cue_pool.py TARGET runs the comparison and cue_pool_check.py verifies the raw results under mac.sh. No default policy changed.

### Learning the relative acoustic weight of an internal ending

Fit the internal-ending and final-ending acoustic costs relative to stem costs in four-part connected decoding. Search weights 1, 2, .5, 4, .25 independently. Select by both endings correct, then by full sequence, then first grid order, on eight old 193-wpm phrases under leave-one-recording-out enrollment and three timing transforms (1,.8,1.25). Also fit one tied ending weight. Freeze before rendering a new 227-wpm panel, old held stems in cyclic offset two, all four number combinations, identity and exponents 2/3 and 1.5. Compare against unit weights with exactly the same enrollment and candidates. Primary: at least four more first endings correct over 64 warped hearings and at most one clean first-ending loss and one final-ending loss across the 96 hearings per voice. Report full lexical accuracy, ties and matched stem-pair uncertainty. No external grammatical evidence at test.

Neither fitted weighting met the target. Both independent optima were unique on the two source criteria: Samantha internal/final weights 1/.5 (21/24 both endings vs 20/24 unit), Joelle .5/2 (15/24 vs 14/24). First ending on clean/early-warp/late-warp panels, each /32: Samantha fitted 27/27/25, tied and unit 27/28/25; Joelle fitted 21/26/23, tied 21/24/22, unit 28/26/25. Paired gains over 64 warped hearings −1 [−4,2] and −2 [−8,5] using eight-pair bootstrap. Every arm preserved 96/96 final endings. Entire four-part strings: Samantha 0/96 in every arm, Joelle 1/96 fitted and tied vs 0/96 unit. Unit-search decisions were checked against the registered kernel for every training and test hearing; a separate forward dynamic program reproduced all three arms on six sampled test hearings per voice. Local external ending_weight.py VOICE fit/test, ending_weight_check.py and saved per-hearing results via mac.sh. Extra signal from weighting did not transfer to these new recordings.

Follow-up diagnostic on the opened panel: freeze unit costs and rerun registered decoding with either the first, the second, or both stem identities supplied while both endings remain unknown. If identifying the first stem were sufficient to fix the timing ambiguity, supplying it alone should recover at least half the original internal-ending errors without spoiling more than one correct hearing per voice. Report rescued and spoiled decisions separately. This is an evaluator intervention and does not supply a live recognition path.

The first-stem intervention failed the sufficiency test. Correct first endings among 96 hearings (free / known first / known second / both): Samantha 80/81/86/80, Joelle 79/74/77/77. Known first rescued 6 and spoiled 5 on Samantha, rescued 8 and spoiled 13 on Joelle. Both known rescued/spoiled 7/7 and 7/9; every final ending stayed correct. Thus even exact noun identity does not generally restore the internal ending with the present acoustic templates and segmentation objective. Every free reading matched the earlier saved hearing. External ending_identity.py VOICE reproduces the four matched registered acoustic calls and stores all decoded sequences. There are 32 physical synthetic recordings per voice and three feature timing conditions per recording. Promising next evidence would be direct grounded examples of the ending in its internal acoustic context, varied independently of neighboring stem identity.

### Learning an internal ending from contrastive noun-pair lessons

Freeze 16 connected lessons per voice at 199 wpm: eight old first stems, each with both first endings; paired lessons fix the second noun and ending. For a matched unpaired curriculum shift the second noun by four places only for the second label, preserving marginal counts. Grounded first-number labels are supplied as two anonymous alternatives, no word boundaries or stem identities for the contrast listener. Convert each whole phrase to 24 time-normalized acoustic samples. Choose a contiguous window (3,6,12 or 24 samples) on the original source recordings by leave-one-first-stem-out binary accuracy, then standardized signed margin. Fit a linear direction between the two training centroids within the window. Compare separate window selection on paired/unpaired lessons, whole-wave centroids, the established connected decoder, and paired label inversion. At 223 wpm use held stems 8–15, cyclic offset six of the established permutation, both endings factorial. Require ≥3/32 improvement in first-ending accuracy over both matched unpaired and established decoding on clean recordings per voice, ≥12/16 complete first-ending contrasts for each voice. Score both time warps separately and preserve final-ending evaluation via the frozen decoder. Costs include both lessons per target stem in training. No target fitting.

Opened results: on clean recordings paired/unpaired/established first-ending correctness was 26/26/26 out of 32 Samantha and 29/28/27 Joelle. Complete first-ending contrasts 10/10/10 and 13/12/12 out of 16. Neither passed the required incremental effect. Both paired listeners chose samples 7–9 (zero-based); on timing exponents 2/3 and 1.5 they fell to 14/18 and 16/19 correct respectively, with only 0/2 and 2/3 complete contrasts. Follow-up timing diagnostic: apply the known inverse feature warp then reuse the frozen paired listener. This uses evaluator timing information. Report restored clean decisions separately from recovered correct answers.

Undoing timing distortion restored 32/32 original clean decisions per distortion Samantha and 31/32 per distortion Joelle; first ending then correct on 26/32 and 30/32 respectively for each warp. The diagnostic localizes the main distorted-panel loss to cue timing. A live listener would have to infer alignment. Whole-wave paired centroids scored 27/32 clean for both voices; the established decoder preserved all 96 final endings per voice. Inverting the paired labels yielded 6/32 and 3/32 clean. Clean paired gains over unpaired with bootstrap intervals over eight held noun pairs: 0 [0,0] and +1 [−4,6]; over the established decoder 0 [−4,4] and +2 [0,5]. Checked every margin by independently comparing squared distances to both centroids on reconstructed features. Each voice contributed 32 physical test recordings, and each curriculum 16 training recordings, with eight recordings shared between curricula. Held stems had earlier bare exemplars in the established decoder; none occurred in the contrast training phrases. Speech was synthesized locally. The window reader ran externally on registered acoustic features with supplied number labels; established decoding used the registered service. Reproduce via mac.sh contrast_ending.py VOICE fit/test, contrast_ending_check.py, contrast_ending_time.py in the external dual-role-word archive. No default change.

### Aligning a number cue from surrounding acoustic context

Reuse frozen 199-wpm paired-lesson classifier. At run time sample the incoming envelope at 24 fractional times raised to a candidate exponent (nine evenly spaced log2 exponents between −1 and +1, preferred tie identity). Select by mean square discrepancy to the label-blind mean teaching trajectory, excluding the classifier window and one sample to either side. Apply the original classifier on that alignment. Compare fixed clock, nearest teaching trajectory on same context, registered four-part decoder and known inverse clock (diagnostic). Freeze before new 229-wpm recordings, same eight held stems, cyclic offset four, both endings factorial. Use true identity, exponents .70/1.45 (off grid), and two independently scored smooth clocks t ± .12 sin(2πt), outside the candidate family. Require +8/64 first-ending decisions over fixed on power clocks and ≤2 clean losses per voice; on smooth clocks require +4/64 separately. Report complete swapped-ending pairs, registered comparator, clock-location error against the known transform and a counterfactual reassigning selected clocks to the opposite distortion of the identical underlying phrase. No clean target reference, distortion ID, second hearing or feedback available to live policies.

First-ending accuracy on the two power clocks improved by +17/64 Samantha and +8/64 Joelle relative to fixed timing. Clean accuracy fell by 3/32 and 6/32. On the two smooth clocks accuracy fell by 10/64 and 11/64. Diagnostic on this opened panel: restrict the evaluator to the exact same nine available alignments, choose the alignment minimizing median absolute source-time discrepancy across the three cue positions using the known transform, and compare its classification with the full inverse and live choice. The evaluator never optimizes on recognition labels. This separates inadequacy of the candidate clocks near the cue from selection using surrounding speech.

Live/fixed/registered power-clock correct Samantha 52/35/51, Joelle 45/37/47 (each /64). Using the other distortion's selected alignment gave 33/64 and 35/64. Paired eight-stem bootstrap intervals for live-minus-fixed were [7,27] and [3,13]. Clean live/fixed 27/30 and 21/27. Smooth-clock live/fixed/registered 32/42/46 and 37/48/42, intervals for live-minus-fixed [−17,−1] and [−21,−1]. Complete paired-ending contrasts, live vs fixed (clean, power, smooth): Samantha 11/14 of 16, 20/6 and 3/12 of 32; Joelle 5/11, 13/9 and 9/16. The alternative nearest-example method scored 51/47 on power and 32/33 on smooth for Samantha/Joelle. The full inverse scored 60/55 on power and 60/56 on smooth. Restricting the evaluator to the nine available candidates and choosing by cue timing alone still yielded 60/56 on power and 56/54 on smooth. The live selection agreed exactly with that restricted evaluator on only 5/64 and 3/64 smooth hearings.
