Mean original-time error across the cue's three sample positions on the two smooth clocks grew from .096 of the phrase for fixed timing to .188 and .176 for live alignment. Whole-phrase consistency poorly localized the cue when different parts moved in different directions. Neither voice met the combined retention and transfer criteria.

The two voices each contribute 32 fresh locally synthesized utterances; timing variants are feature-space transforms. The external listener used the stored training trajectories without their labels for clock selection; the registered connected decoder remained a separate comparator. Independently reconstructed all 320 alignments, nearest-template choices and signed decisions for every candidate from WAV-derived features using linear interpolation and distance differences. External cue_clock.py VOICE, cue_clock_check.py and cue_clock_local.py via mac.sh reproduce the saved decisions, scores and evaluator-only comparisons. No default changed.

### Local landmarks for the internal number ending

Freeze the prior classifier and nine alignment candidates. Use mean training acoustics at the two samples on each side of the frozen three-sample window. Compare (i) minimum local discrepancy, (ii) local discrepancy plus a learned identity-clock preference, (iii) distant samples only with the same fitting opportunity, (iv) previous whole-context policy, (v) identity and (vi) registered four-part decoder. Normalize candidate discrepancy by its across-candidate mean, then add lambda times absolute log2 exponent. Select lambda in 0,.05,.15,.4,1,3 by least mean absolute cue-location error using old 199-wpm teaching phrases with leave-one-stem-out centroids and generated timing interventions: identity twice, powers .8 and 1.25, smooth perturbations ±.08 sine. No number labels used to fit the locator; simulated source timing is supervision. Primary fresh 221-wpm test uses held eight stems in cyclic offset one, both endings crossed, transformations identity, .70/1.45 powers, ±.12 sine; never expose transform to locator. Per voice require +6/64 smooth-clock first endings over each of fixed and previous whole-context policy, ≤2 clean losses vs fixed and ≤3 power-clock losses vs previous whole-context. Report membership of both halves of each ending pair, comparisons with fitted distant context, signed changes and sampling uncertainty. Independently test timing localization, including swapping selected alignments between opposite distortions.

Both local fits chose lambda 3 and kept identity on all 160 hearings per voice. To distinguish weak local features from mismatched reference material, run a separate privileged-reference diagnostic on the same hearings, with no identity penalty. Compare unlabelled clean reference phrases from the same test voice: (a) same two nouns and final ending but opposite first ending, and (b) cyclically next noun pair with the same opposing first ending and final ending. Use only the four local reference positions for alignment, never its classifier-window samples or number label. These references require additional acoustic material and evaluator-selected pairings. Compare realized accuracy and geometric timing errors to the original population-mean context.

Clean / power / smooth first-ending counts (denominators 32/64/64): Samantha fitted local and fixed 30/37/44, unpenalized local 23/44/34, fitted distant 30/37/44, previous whole context 23/49/33, registered decoder 24/48/42; Joelle fitted local and fixed 29/34/40, unpenalized local 20/38/41, fitted distant 29/34/40, previous whole context 24/46/38, registered 22/49/40. Fitted local complete pairs: 14/16, 5/32, 12/32 Samantha and 13/16, 3/32, 8/32 Joelle. Swapping the chosen clock produced identical results because every selection was identity. Smooth net vs whole context +11 [2,20] and +2 [−6,12], but zero vs fixed; power loss −12 [−18,−5] and −12 [−22,−2]. Intervals resample eight noun pairs. Both missed the primary. No selective realignment demonstrated.

Reference diagnostic: matching local samples from the same lexical context with an opposing first ending vs another pair improved mean cue-position error on power (.079 vs .148, .061 vs .136) and smooth (.093 vs .135, .079 vs .143) for Samantha and Joelle respectively, in units of phrase duration. Smooth accuracy same/other was 41/34 and 42/31, power 44/38 and 52/34. Even this matched context on clean speech scored only 21/32 and 26/32 vs fixed 30/32 and 29/32. The reference thus contains useful landmarks, but the alignment can also move when only the ending changes. The matched extra reference was supplied in this diagnostic and unavailable in the primary evaluation. Verify with ending_landmark.py VOICE fit/test, ending_landmark_check.py and ending_landmark_ref.py via mac.sh in the external dual-role-word archive. Recomputed the 320 held cost vectors from locally synthesized recordings, including nearest preceding/following samples and training means. Only 32 distinct test recordings per voice; distortions act on acoustic features. No deployment change.

### Retrieving an unlabelled acoustic memory to time a spoken inflection

Freeze one chronological archive of eight 201-wpm recordings per voice, one per held noun pair at cyclic offset three, first ending i modulo two and final ending floor(i/2) modulo two. Archive both noun combinations and their pronunciation but supply no labels, handles or test feedback to retrieval. Render all four target endings at 235 wpm separately. Only one quarter repeat archival lexical content, all target recordings are new. Use mean squared acoustic distance on 48 reference samples for monotone dynamic time warping with diagonal, horizontal, vertical steps and .15 feature-cost penalty per non-diagonal step. Omit reference samples falling within the frozen first-ending window (including half-sample margins); linearly interpolate alignment between adjacent visible anchors. Rank eight references by total path cost, map the three cue times and select the nearest of the frozen nine clocks. Compare retrieval, forced same pair, cyclic reassignment of the selected reference, fixed clock, previous whole-context alignment and registered connected decoder. Freeze primary endpoints: ≥75% correct noun-pair retrieval including when first ending changes, ≥5/64 more smooth-clock correct than fixed and than reassigned reference, ≤2 clean losses vs fixed, independently per voice. Report paired power/smooth outcomes, correct pair vs accuracy under changed endings, alternative when the right reference is withheld and reverse-order invariance. The assigned clock family and acoustic classifier are supplied. This tests an external acoustic store; storage is eight reference feature arrays and retrieval work counts even on clean cases.

Opened audit: every incorrect retrieval chose a reference whose final ending matched the query (73/73 Samantha, 58/58 Joelle). Same-pair retrieval was 80/80 and 79/80 if the final ending matched the archived one, only 7/80 and 23/80 if it differed. Diagnostic on this opened panel: exclude the last third of the reference trajectory from retrieval cost as well as the earlier cue mask, keeping the original per-reference clocks exactly fixed. Compare changed-final vs unchanged-final success and subsequent first-ending choices. This positional mask is evaluator-designed after inspecting these failures.

Primary acoustic retrieval recovered the noun pair on 87/160 and 102/160 hearings. On changed first endings: 40/80 and 41/80 correct retrievals, with 53/80 and 47/80 correct first-number decisions. For clean / power / smooth clocks, correct first-number choices respectively: Samantha retrieved 25/47/43, forced same pair 27/52/47, reassigned 23/43/41, fixed 27/37/46, whole context 25/48/32, right pair withheld 23/41/39, registered decoder 26/51/44. Joelle retrieved 25/51/42, forced 26/49/47, reassigned 24/41/44, fixed 27/38/44, whole context 21/42/34, withheld 22/41/33, registered 22/38/36. Denominators 32/64/64; complete first-ending pairs for retrieved policy 9/15/12 and 9/19/13 of 16/32/32. Power gains vs fixed +10 [1,18] and +13 [3,23]; smooth −3 [−8,2] and −2 [−15,10]. Intervals resample eight noun pairs. Primary missed retrieval and smooth improvement thresholds. Payload 39,936 bytes per voice. Local median/p95 time for archive alignment and ranking only was 13.4/18.5 ms and 13.0/16.6 ms, measured during concurrent voice evaluations; excludes feature extraction and synthesis.

Post hoc tail masking raised same-pair retrieval with changed final endings to 70/80 and 63/80 while reducing unchanged-final to 78/80 and 71/80. First-ending accuracy clean/power/smooth became 26/51/47 and 23/46/44. Better lexical retrieval helped Samantha overall but hurt Joelle (118→113/160). Even the supplied correct reference attained 47/64 smooth on each voice. Retrieving an appropriate phrase and timing the inflection within it need separate tests. Each voice had eight unlabelled archived renditions and 32 distinct new test recordings; inverse order selected identical references throughout, and SHA-256 hashes of all 40 WAVs were distinct per voice. Independently rebuilt path costs on five hearings per voice × all eight references and checked all stored alternative selections. Reproduce with memory_cue.py VOICE, memory_cue_check.py and memory_cue_tail.py through mac.sh in the external dual-role-word archive. Archive lookup and interpolation are external, the four-part acoustic comparator uses registered scoring. No default or deployment change.

### Learning which acoustic regions matter for a retrieval request

Two anonymous request handles are grounded by demonstrations selecting a recording: match both noun identities or match the final ending. Source and held vocabularies are disjoint. Cross four possible first nouns with two second nouns. Each archived pair has a checkerboard final ending; target queries change that ending. For each query offer the matching noun pair and one other phrase that matches the new final ending; foils share either the first or second noun. The same public recordings and menu thus require opposite selections for the two handles. Use both first endings, counterbalance menu order, and evaluate neutral panels where the matchers can agree separately. No word boundaries, noun IDs or ending bits go into acoustic distances. Distance vector: eight equal time bins, each using the cheapest local time offset of −4 to +4 on a 48-sample grid and a squared discrepancy. Fit two simplex-weighted distances by binary pointer loss with .01 quadratic regularization toward uniform, using only clean, .8 and 1.25 power-clock source examples. Controls: shared uniform, fixed first-two-thirds and final-third rules, exchanged fits, and the best source-selected single bin per request. Freeze before testing 207/233 wpm archived/query rates, held nouns and distortions 1, .70, 1.45, ±.12 sine. Require ≥75% both-request success per voice for each foil relation and ≥10 percentage points over the fixed paired region rules overall. Separately report clean and distorted, transfer to equal-ending menus and bootstrap across lexical pairs. No claim about inferring the spoken request itself: handles and demonstrations are supplied.

The primary noun metric favored early samples; both voices passed the shared-second foil requirement but failed shared-first. Opened-panel diagnostic: fit the identical noun metric separately on source menus with the first noun shared and menus with the second noun shared. Supply the test foil class only to this evaluator and compare with the original single noun metric. This tests whether source supervision specific to the distinction suffices. The ending metric and its evidence remain fixed.

Both voices located the final ending on all 160/160 conflict menus and all 160/160 neutral menus per voice. Ending fits concentrated ≥84% of weight in bin seven of eight. On conflict menus the complete two-request score therefore equaled noun accuracy: Samantha 45/80 when the first noun was shared vs 75/80 when the second was shared, Joelle 42/80 vs 74/80. Clean / power / smooth joint counts Samantha 24/32,48/64,48/64; Joelle 25/32,46/64,45/64. Overall learned / fixed paired regions / independently selected single bins: 120/122/119 of 160 Samantha, 116/102/97 Joelle. Paired gain over fixed regions −1.25 points [−13.75,11.25] and +8.75 [−10.625,30], resampling eight noun pairs. Swapping the two fits yielded 0/160 both voices on conflict menus; the request-independent metric necessarily failed joint conflict but gave 160/160 and 158/160 joint neutral. Learned neutral joint 119/160 and 142/160. Primary failed on both voices. A control had an exact acoustic distance tie; the verifier counts tied decisions (absolute margin ≤1e−12) as unresolved and checks reversal on all decisive menus.

When the evaluator supplied which noun differed, source fits used bins five/six for a differing second noun and one/two for a differing first noun. Shared-first accuracy rose from 45 to 63/80 Samantha and 42 to 51/80 Joelle; shared-second was 74/80 and 75/80. This localizes some loss to using one noun metric regardless of what alternatives share; Joelle still had substantial acoustic errors even given that information. Six source and six held stem identities per voice, 16 source query recordings and 32 held, eight additional archive recordings per split. Each of 96 source menus had two task pointers and reuses recordings across foil choices and timing variants. All forty held WAVs were bytewise distinct and disjoint from the 24 source WAVs within voice. Independently recalculated sampled block distances from waveforms, both target pointers from reference identities, and all paired contrasts from saved margins. The acoustic features were obtained locally, and learned retrieval and pointing ran as external numerical replay with supplied handles. Reproduce retrieval_focus.py VOICE fit/test, retrieval_focus_audio.py, retrieval_focus_check.py and retrieval_focus_relation.py via mac.sh in dual-role-word. No default deployment change.

### Conditioning acoustic attention on the offered alternatives

Freeze a source-pointer-only model for the noun request. Its eight bin weights are a softmax of a learned bias plus a linear projection of eight symmetric distances between the two reference recordings. No reference IDs, order, noun labels, foil class or query timestamps enter the projection. Standardize each contextual feature using source mean and variance with minimum scale .1. Fit on the same archived 96 pointer menus per voice with logistic pointer loss, .005 mean squared parameter penalty, zero initialization, no held tuning. Fit a bias-only model identically and retain the previously frozen simplex noun and ending models. Freeze a fresh vocabulary assignment (first nouns 12–15, second 8–9), rates 209/239, archive first ending alternating by column, checkerboard final ending, same conflict and neutral comparisons and clocks. Primary on conflict: ≥75% noun choices for both foil relations per voice, ≥8/160 extra correct vs stronger of original and bias-only fixed fits, and ≥6/160 extra vs exchanging just the two context vectors on the identical query. Report joint success on both menus of a query, neutral trials, ending retention and history-clustered uncertainty. Compare a nonlearned attention multiplier based on reference contrast. Keep source/held labels strictly in training/evaluation.

Samantha passed the three primary point thresholds (70/80 and 73/80; +20/160 over stronger fixed; +82/160 over exchanged context). Joelle scored 45/80 and 62/80, tying the stronger fixed on total choices. Failure diagnostic on the opened data: transplant each fitted model onto the other voice's same saved acoustic comparisons, standardizing the receiving context by its own source mean and variance. Compare with evaluator-supplied foil routing to the two source-trained relation-specific metrics (and the best single source bin per relation). These tests separate the ability to infer what distinguishes the menu from the ability to hear the noun at the location supplied. No held-target fit.

Conflict choices (context / exchanged / bias / old static / contrast multiplier): Samantha 143/61/113/123/122 of 160, Joelle 107/100/106/107/109. Both alternative menus correct for the same utterance on 65/80 Samantha and 41/80 Joelle, versus 10/80 and 34/80 exchanged. Relative to the original static fit, paired eight-lexical-pair bootstrap gains +20 [8,31] and 0 [−7,8]; vs exchanging only the descriptors +82 [66,96] and +7 [−22,36]. Neutral query noun scores 150/160 and 114/160. Ending reader conflict accuracy 160/160 and 146/160, neutral 160/160 and 158/160. Identical query and menu-order reversal preserved all decisive selections by symmetric context and antisymmetric query score. Samantha assigned mean .97 weight to bin five when the first noun was shared and .93 to bins one and two when the second was shared. Joelle allocated .75 to the first two bins even when the first noun was shared.

On Joelle, supplying the distinction to the previously fitted source relation-specific metrics raised shared-first accuracy from 45 to 65/80, with shared-second unchanged at 62/80. The Samantha attention fit on Joelle yielded only 44/70 of the respective 80 decisions; Joelle's fit transferred to Samantha yielded 47/73. For Samantha the supplied routing yielded 69/77. Thus the positive causal listening effect is specific to one synthetic voice in this experiment. Both tasks reuse recordings over timing variants, with eight archived noun pairs and 32 fresh query WAVs per voice. Each comparison used a supplied request handle; contrast-dependent attention is an external numerical listener using local acoustic features. Validation independently reconstructed softmax weights from saved coefficients and all signed choices, and replayed sampled waveform distances and reversed contexts. Use menu_attention.py VOICE fit/test, menu_attention_check.py and menu_attention_transfer.py via mac.sh in the external dual-role-word archive. No default change.

### Breaking a teaching correlation between inflection and noun contrast

In the original source menu, archive first endings matched exactly when the first noun was shared; in the last target archive they matched exactly when the second noun was shared. Test this as a potential shortcut. Both teaching policies now hear the same 16 reference WAVs (both first endings per noun pair) and same queries and receive equal numbers of pointers. Correlated curriculum repeats original match/differ relation twice, with both absolute ending values balanced; crossed curriculum independently varies both reference endings. No hidden relation or ending bit is a model feature. Use identical source-only optimization and separately fit bias-only baseline on crossed data. At test use fresh 213/241-wpm recordings, first noun order 14,12,15,13 and second nouns 9,8, all four reference ending assignments for each query/foil; query final ending conflicts with the target noun-pair archive. Both query first endings, both foil relations and clean/power/smooth clocks. Primary per voice: crossed beats equal-exposure correlated by ≥5 percentage points on reversed-correlation menus, retains original-correlation accuracy within 3 points and reaches ≥75% per foil relation. Report both-curricula clean paired effects and uncertainty across eight noun pairs. Measure how often changing only the distractor's first ending changes the noun decision. Exchanged-context and fixed-weight controls separate context use from better static weights. No extra fitting after opening the two test panels.

Attribution check fixed before reading held scores: fit one bias-only model per actual foil relation on crossed source menus; evaluator supplies that relation at test. Compare its decisions with live crossed fitting. For each held choice, also count how often every one of the eight individual bins favors the wrong noun or ties: no nonnegative mixture of the supplied evidence can then succeed. Report this hindsight ceiling only as a diagnostic.

On reversed-correlation menus the crossed/correlated scores were 269/243 of 320 Samantha (+8.13 points, paired eight-pair bootstrap gain [6,46] decisions) and 234/221 Joelle (+4.06 points, [3,24]). Original-correlation scores were 276/267 and 255/261, within the retention margin. Shared-first/shared-second results under crossed teaching were 262/283 of 320 Samantha and 214/275 Joelle. Samantha passed all three point criteria, Joelle missed reversed gain and shared-first accuracy. Changing only the distractor first ending caused 49 vs 92 answer flips across 320 matched Samantha pairs and 53 vs 80 Joelle (crossed vs correlated); correct for both endings on 248 vs 209 and 218 vs 201. Both foil menus correct on 233 vs 214 and 191 vs 184 of 320. On clean hearings crossed/correlated scored 121/110 and 115/106 of 128. Overall crossed/static-bias/exchanged-context: 545/474/211 of 640 Samantha and 489/448/255 Joelle. Pretraining exposure audit confirmed identical query counts and equal per-recording archive exposures; the correlated curriculum has half as many unique pairings, each repeated twice. This estimates the benefit of changing the pairings at a fixed presentation count, not an inherent disadvantage of redundant teaching in every setting.

With supplied foil class, fixed source relation metrics scored 273/260 of 320 by foil relation on Samantha and 238/270 on Joelle. In 315/320 Joelle shared-first choices at least one bin favored the right pair; that hindsight count does not give the agent a deployable selector. The costly residual error is concentrated where the second noun differs; even correct routing with a static learned within-relation metric achieved only 74.4% there. The 640 decisions per voice reuse 16 new query WAVs and 16 reference WAVs across ending assignments, foils and five timings. Readings are synthetic and whole-phrase hearing runs as external replay on local acoustic features. No new default. Reproduce crossed_focus.py VOICE fit/test, crossed_focus_check.py and crossed_focus_limit.py under mac.sh in external dual-role-word; sample waveform scores reconstructed, alternative assignments and sources verified.

### Teaching equivalent lexical references with different final inflections

Freeze the crossed noun-attention model. Keep canonical 213-wpm reference phrases and first-ending assignment u=i%2; choose one additional 203-wpm rendition per pair, either repeating the same ending or changing only the final ending. Explicit shared lexical-reference binding is supplied by teaching; no segment boundaries or ending labels go into noun recognition. Use attention from the two canonical recordings in all arms, score each offered referent by its closest complete exemplar under that shared weight vector. Single canonical, matched repeat, changed ending and wrong-referent enrollment (cycle to the other second noun) controls. Queries are new 237-wpm recordings with all endings and the same held noun pairs, both foil relations and the previous five clocks. For each voice require ≥8 extra correct out of 160 over repeats on conflict/shared-first, ≤4 losses out of 160 on neutral/shared-first, and ≥10 extra complete contrasts out of 160 across both query-final-ending forks, compared with repeats on shared-first (actual complete contrasts denominator is 80 per relation: 8 pairs × 2 query first endings × 5 clocks; require +10/80). Report other relation, canonical ending-request accuracy and physical audio seconds; separate cost of the provided reference binding and three archive choices. No fitting or purchasing policy in this study.

Pre-run denominator correction: each shared-first stratum also has 80, since fixing conflict or neutral fixes the query final ending. Retain the absolute gain requirement of eight extra conflict choices (+8/80) and the loss-rate margin of 2.5% (at most two neutral losses out of 80). All three corrected criteria apply before generating query results.

On the shared-first conflict panel changed-vs-repeat improved Joelle by 14/80 and Samantha by 7/80, while Samantha lost four matched-final-ending choices. Attribution fork on saved hearings: substitute changed-ending teaching for the intended candidate only, for the distractor only, or for both. These asymmetric assignments require the correct candidate and are evaluator diagnostics. Decompose changed decisions into rescued and spoiled for each condition.

Each cell contains 80 hearings. Shared-first conflict, neutral and both-ending complete pairs (single / repeat / changed / wrong-referent enrollment): Samantha 62/64/71/9, 75/76/72/66 and 62/64/70/8; Joelle 46/46/60/17, 59/59/59/65 and 45/45/59/16. Primary point criteria passed on Joelle only. Paired eight-noun-pair bootstrap intervals for changed-minus-repeat complete-pair gains were [−1,15] and [4,24]. Joelle's conflict gains all came from corrections with zero new errors; replacing the distractor's enrollment alone yielded one rescue and one loss. On Samantha the intended-only change rescued 10/80 with zero losses on conflicts, but changing both rescued nine and spoiled two. On her neutral cases changing both rescued one and spoiled five; giving additional pronunciations to a distractor can make it spuriously attractive.

On shared-second foils, repeat vs changed complete pairs were 72/73 Samantha, 68/68 Joelle. Canonical ending-request complete pairs shared-first/shared-second: 80/78 and 73/80 respectively; this readout used only the canonical inventory and tests no new learning. Canonical/repeat/changed audio duration (seconds): 8.57/8.96/9.01 and 9.00/9.38/9.44. Binding is supplied for eight new exemplars per arm. Each voice had 32 fresh query WAVs and 24 archive WAVs, all hashes distinct, with transformed and paired decisions reusing them. No generalization to an unobserved suffix follows; the benefit concerns a demonstrated variant at a different speech rate. Checked ties, all full-exemplar costs and sampled waveform comparisons against an independent decision reconstruction. Reproduce lexical_takes.py VOICE, lexical_takes_check.py and lexical_takes_forks.py via mac.sh in dual-role-word. Local synthetic speech features and external numerical reference lookup. No default change.

### Binding pronunciation memories across shuffled ambiguous scenes

Canonical 213-wpm forms retain their original eight reference identities. Teach changed-final forms at 203 and 217 wpm in two separately shuffled rounds. Anonymous persistent object handles are supplied; each scene shows two unordered candidate handles, no pointer or cross-round utterance ID. Round one shows the two same-first-noun competitors. In the informative round two, either the old referent or its competitor persists, paired with a third handle outside that pair. Compare ordinary and consistently swapped alternate-form conventions with identical audio, first scenes, orders and test menus. In redundant round two the same two handles persist and the balanced convention is unidentifiable. Use symmetric acoustic distances to choose each first recording's nearest second rendition without scene labels, intersect the candidate sets and store its original acoustic exemplar only if the intersection is unique. Compare minimum-total-distance bijective matching (separate supplied once-per-round prior), oracle utterance correspondence, first-scene-only abstention, forced acoustic nearest canonical and direct teacher binding. Hold canonical query readout and frozen attention fixed. Primary: at least 7/8 correct bindings in each convention per voice and ≥75% correct in BOTH conventions for the very same altered-form test query on fresh 243-wpm speech (two first endings × eight pairs × five clocks); ≥20-point joint gain vs redundant history. Also require canonical-form accuracy loss ≤3 points vs no writes in either convention. Report false writes in ambiguous histories, exact matching gaps, and separately whether the reference or acoustic readout limited performance. This tests supplied cross-situational intersection and a bounded lexicon, not discovery of object permanence or a learning rule.

All 8/8 cross-round recordings were independently paired correctly in both voices. With informative scenes all eight bindings followed each convention, and redundant second scenes produced zero writes. To distinguish post-binding errors, give the evaluator the correct bank (canonical or changed-final) but no noun identity, then classify by the same stored distances solely within that bank. When the alternate bank by itself is wrong, gating alone cannot repair the decision. This is privileged routing on the opened panel.

The independent nearest matcher equaled the bijective and exact-link arms. Minimum acoustic gap to the next candidate was .712 Samantha and 1.025 Joelle; exhaustive enumeration of 8! assignments agreed with the bijective solution. Acoustic-only nearest canonical stored 0/8 correctly under either Samantha convention, 3/8 and 0/8 Joelle. On 80 altered-form queries per voice, identical audio/display correct under both scene conventions: Samantha 72/80 (ordinary and remapped each 73/80); Joelle 50/80 (60/80 and 62/80). Redundant teaching, forced nearest canonical and no writes each scored 0/80 joint for both voices. Eight-pair bootstrap intervals for learned joint counts [64,78] and [32,66]. Samantha passed the 75% altered-form threshold; Joelle missed it. Canonical queries ordinary/remapped: Samantha 72/66 vs 76/76 with no writes, failing retention; Joelle 59/65 vs 59/59, passing the point margin. Neither voice satisfied all criteria. Direct teacher binding gave exactly the inferred memory decisions.

Correct-bank diagnostic gave altered-form joint 73/80 and 57/80, rescuing one and seven previously missed pairs; even perfect bank choice is insufficient for Joelle under current within-bank acoustic scoring. For canonical forms the same diagnostic restored 17 and five joint decisions lost under open competition, reaching 76/80 and 59/80. This isolates retention interference after correct grounding. Candidate handles and truthful scene inclusion were supplied, repeat tokens were separately synthesized examples of exactly the same phrase, and each voice contributed only 16 distinct altered-form query WAVs (plus 16 canonical) replayed over five clocks. Two ambiguous scene observations per new archive item, no paid lookup policy. Verified all 56 waveform hashes per voice distinct, reconstructed intersection bindings, assignment and readout scores and matched-world choices. External scene_lex.py VOICE, scene_lex_check.py and scene_lex_gate.py via mac.sh. No change to the default runtime.

### Transferring a speaker's cue choice to untaught alternate forms

Withhold both alternate forms of a whole lexical pair at fitting and at decision time. Retain its two familiar canonical recordings. Other three lexical pairs each supply two alternate examples bound by the prior two-scene acoustic intersection learner. On those six receipts, select a convex mixture of the two established anonymous request scorers (noun-pair and final-ending match), candidates 0,.1,…,1 ending weight, by correct referent choices then mean signed normalized margin, tie favors noun. Reference-to-query bin distances and canonical menu comparison remain fixed; model gets only public handles, archived waveforms and inferred pointed choices. Test at fresh 247-wpm, both query first endings, all four held pairs in rotation, all five timing conditions. Compare to no alt enrollment noun-only, ending-only, equal mixture, and all-pairs enrolled nearest-exemplar control (extra target teaching). In each voice require ≥75% both-world correct on 80 altered-form queries, ≥15 extra joint decisions vs a static mixture, and ≤2/80 loss in each convention on unchanged forms vs noun-only. Also evaluate correct on three matched inputs (canonical plus alternate under both conventions), matched teaching reversal, and a hidden independent pair-specific exception where transfer cannot anticipate the switch. The possible scorers are supplied; no new grammar or acoustic segmentation induced.

All four leave-pair-out fits on both voices selected noun-only in the ordinary convention and ending-only in the remapped convention. This happened from six bound examples, without target-pair alternate exemplars. Recomputing cross-round nearest correspondence after excluding both target alternates from both rounds produced identical choices in every fold. The learned listener answered the identical alternate-form query correctly in both conventions on 63/80 Samantha and 41/80 Joelle. By timing condition (clean, .70, 1.45, plus, minus), joint counts of 16: 14/10/13/14/12 and 12/6/11/8/4. Individual ordinary/remapped altered-form scores 63/80 and 80/80 Samantha, 46/80 and 73/80 Joelle. Fixed noun, fixed ending and fixed equal mixture necessarily scored zero joint on these contradictory fork labels. Changing only the teaching selections exchanged the two fitted scoring rules. Canonical-form scores ordinary/remapped 74/80 and 80/80 Samantha and 60/80 and 80/80 Joelle, preserving the noun-only baseline in both. Both canonical readings AND both altered readings correct on 62/80 and 41/80 matched sets. The all-enrolled memory reference scored 69/80 and 51/80 joint altered but only 57/80 and 46/80 complete four-reading sets. Samantha passed all point criteria, Joelle missed altered-form joint comprehension. Fitting found the same cue distinction on both voices; acoustic noun discrimination is the binding limit in Joelle's ordinary branch.

For an unannounced independent reversal confined to the held pair, the public teaching history is identical: before distinguishing evidence the same decisions scored 0/80 and 2/80 joint under the reversed pair. With balanced probability of the local exception, unchanged and exception labels are complements and expected individual correctness is exactly .5. This underscores the supplied regularity of the positive fixture. Eight-lexical-item resampling on the fixed panels gave joint count intervals [52,74] and [34,47]; items share menus in four folds, so these intervals do not quantify independent-language or independent-speaker uncertainty. Per voice there were 32 new physical query WAVs, five feature timings and two counterfactual teaching sets. ### Learning an unordered object lexicon from whole phrase–scene pairs

Six familiar isolated acoustic stem exemplars (IDs 8–13) are supplied without visual bindings. Each teaching scene exposes two unordered persistent object handles, no assigned spoken order. Use six edges of a cycle as teaching combinations (each object in two scenes), reversed spoken order on alternate edges and deterministic balanced ending variations. Enroll the inferred best reading of each teaching phrase; no isolated object pointers. Reserve the nine remaining unordered pairs and both spoken orders from all scene teaching. Present two unordered candidate scenes differing in one object; constrain both acoustic orders at readout. Both scenes must be held-out edges. Compare ground-truth bindings with identical enrollment, no context enrollment, and permuted teaching-scene labels. Controls for ambiguity: a lone unordered teaching pair leaves its within-pair swap unknowable; identical audio and scenes must give identical evidence for both assignments. Before synthesis freeze success as ≥5/6 true object bindings per voice and ≥75% complete pairs (both directions at each of two ending patterns) across the nine withheld pairs per voice. Report role of the closed inventory/bijection and acoustic assumptions; separate subset vs all-six mapping and chance controls.

Initial run: Joelle recovered all six bindings but only one of nine complete withheld pairs; Samantha recovered four and passed five of nine complete pairs. Follow-up diagnostics on the opened panel: (i) score all 6^6 mappings, including coincident names, with actual registered repeated-name costs on each of the six teaching phrases; (ii) with exact bindings and context enrollment, measure readout if the two rival scenes must both start with the common object rather than compete in either order. This second fork is evaluator-supplied order information and may move scores either way. Also audit how often a menu-only majority guess could succeed on the finite panel.

Six unordered scene lessons per voice. Bijection search recovered 4/6 Samantha and 6/6 Joelle. Best–runner-up total cost gap .09591 and .11392 respectively. Numbers of exactly optimal candidate dictionaries after each prefix were 48/6/2/1/1/1 for both voices; a unique optimum need not be the true mapping. Swapping a lone pair's assignments gives identical evidence, so a choice between them at that point is unsupported. Without the bijection, unrestricted fit recovered 5/6 and 4/6 respectively, with duplicate names in both cases.

On 36 fresh physically distinct test recordings per voice (nine entirely untaught pairs × both orders × both ending patterns), learned / correct supplied dictionary / no new context enrollment / cyclically permuted scene labels: Samantha 31/31/30/15 individual correct, 5/5/4/0 complete pairs; Joelle 23/23/23/18 and 1/1/1/0. Neither voice passed the joint lexicon-and-composition criteria. Correct dictionary plus supplied common-object-first order still scored 31/36 and 23/36 with 5/9 and 2/9 complete. The main Joelle deficit survived both corrections. Fixed menu generation was unbalanced: a hindsight menu-only lookup could reach 24/36. Restricting to matching unordered menus and ending patterns with opposite correct responses produced 12 two-recording contrasts per voice. Both decisions correct on 9/12 and 3/12 under the learned dictionary, 8/12 and 3/12 with the correct dictionary, and 0/12 each after cyclic label reassignment. These are diagnostic subsets, not a fully balanced confirmatory test.

Target object handles, isolated prototypes, the supplied four-part pattern, two-object scene size and the preferred bijection remain assumptions. The six teaching combinations had no ordered object labels or name pointers; no images were parsed. Acoustic teaching/scoring used local registered calls, mapping search ran externally. Independent enumeration verified all ranked hypotheses and prefix tie counts, disjoint training/test unordered pairs, and distinct hashes across 42 recordings per voice. Bare gavi/tavi: original and expanded four-part bank each recognized all 4/4 isolated bare recordings per voice (two names × two rates); the marked-whole bank has no bare template, so that cell is inapplicable. Two available lesson styles: target and neighbor words with matching or opposite endings. No new training after opening held scores.

For equal-pilot-budget controls also finish the pooled pilot bank with a fixed same or fixed opposed second neighbor. For an illustrative accounting assign 1 unit per complete test pair and .1 unit per labelled teaching or practice recording (excluding common isolated vocabulary/history and wall time). Extra adaptive cost vs fixed mix is 2.4 units per voice; time-to-repayment is identifiable from a positive paired rate only under repeated comparable deployments. Keep raw outcome separate.

Pilot choice selected matching for both. Samantha held benefit vs development-best mix +10 pairs, Joelle −6; Joelle's tie on six practice pairs correct per arm was broken by mean margin. To localize this miss on opened results, evaluate pilot and completed banks on the original biko practice block and an additional nelo practice block at the same 225 wpm, same eight contrast fixtures. Compare rankings by the unchanged rule separately and pooled. Full banks are a hindsight diagnostic of returns to later teaching; nelo adds 16 practice labels, not free evidence. No revised held-out success claim.

On eight practice contrasts opposed/same = 3/5 Samantha, 6/6 Joelle (Joelle individual also tied 14/16, signed margins .0891/.0959). Fresh 32-pair test per voice: same/opposed/mix1 Samantha 29/17/19, Joelle 20/29/26. The chosen style scored 49/64 vs 45/64 development-best mixed, 49/64 retrospective fixed same, 46/64 fixed opposed and 58/64 per-voice style oracle. Aggregate +4 passed the +3 floor, Joelle −6 failed the per-voice loss guard. Differences vs mix1 across four neighbor/rate blocks: +1,0,+3,+6 Samantha; −1,−2,−1,−2 Joelle. Block bootstrap conditional on these four cells gave [+2,+19] and [−8,−4] pairs; two neighbors do not support a speaker-population estimate. Thus keeping pilots mitigated Joelle's miss but an equal-pilot-budget fixed opposed continuation gave 56/64 vs the selected retained 53/64.

At the specified .1 unit per labelled recording, chosen 49−8=41 vs development-best 45−3.2=41.8 units pooled on this panel. Under constant replication mix only, adaptive would need 4.8/(4/64)=76.8 total pairs to repay extra teaching on point estimates; this extrapolation assumes equal voice mix and sustained gain. There is no observed repayment and no possible advantage over always using the identical same-style choice on this panel given positive selection cost. Even on original biko, fully expanded Joelle banks ranked opposed above same (8/6) although pilots tied. ### Buying another practice context only on a tied comparison

Prospective Rishi and Tessa study, neither used to design the practice selector. Reuse frozen name pair, teaching styles, and fees. Pilot on 8 labelled source phrases per style. Serve a first labelled practice block (8 complete contrasts, 16 recordings) with biko or nelo, both presentation orders tested as matched deployments. Buy the other practice block solely when first-block complete contrast counts tie, then select the style using pooled evidence; otherwise stop. Rank by complete contrasts then individual correctness then normalized margin, with exact ties favoring opposed. Fit/serialize choices before access to any held scores. Test at 229/249 wpm, unseen connected neighbors dupo/saro, 32 contrasts per voice. Compare never buying the extra block, always buying it, fixed same, opposed and previously development-selected mix1. Model one reward per complete contrast, fee .1 per labelled recording, 24 teaching and 16 or 32 practice recordings adaptive, 16 teaching for fixed; exclude common audio normalization/isolated inventory separately. Positive value of selective second practice requires at least one actual purchase and one skip, ≥1 net unit improvement on average per deployment versus never, and no voice losing net on average. Report separate whole-policy comparison vs strongest fixed, oracle value of the same optional extra block and actual information available. Do not reinterpret a redundant purchase or zero opportunities as success.

Frozen rule exercised two purchases and two skips over two voices × two matched block orders. Rishi: biko first tied at 5/8 per style; buying nelo kept opposed. Nelo first favored opposed 6/8 vs same 5/8 and stopped. Tessa: biko first favored opposed 8/8 vs 4/8 and stopped. Nelo first tied at 6/8 and margin favored same; buying biko switched to opposed. Tessa's switch therefore cost one correct contrast before fees. With proposed costs, gate-minus-stop on the two paid forks −1.6 Rishi and −2.6 Tessa, on the skipped forks 0. Mean net per 32-pair deployment: gate 21.70 vs never 22.75 and always 20.90. Both fixed styles averaged 24.90, fixed mix 21.90. Selective purchase missed both outcome criteria. A hindsight oracle restricted to choosing whether to purchase that same second practice block also scored 22.75, declining all offers. Even with perfect advance knowledge of the better final style per voice, the greatest gross gain over the stronger pooled fixed style is only .5 unit per deployment at this horizon, below the 2.4-unit initial adaptive overhead alone. No positive break-even for the actual extra-block decisions on this panel; near ties on practice did not imply high downstream value.

Held differences between styles varied across neighbor/rate blocks. Opposed-minus-same Rishi [0,0,0,+1], Tessa [−1,+2,0,−2]. Only two independent new voices and four reused acoustic blocks per voice; the two order forks are not independent additional speakers. Physical synthesis time, model fitting and storage excluded from modeled label fees. ### Asking for the disputed spoken word by its position

Freeze the isolated-marked-form reader and a binary display with one common noun and competing gavi/tavi identities. From the registered four alternative costs (two identities × both orders) compute normalized choice and position margins, infer most likely disputed position. No evaluator position or class at request time. Actions: point immediately (0 fee), hear the whole phrase at 195 wpm (.12), hear the bare first or last word at 187 wpm (.10). Bare replies matched by registered isolated recognition against the three displayed names, using familiar 173-wpm forms; if the common name is heard retain original choice. On whole repeat choose from the sum of normalized initial and repeat signed margins. Learn the action (stop/repeat/ask inferred position/ask opposite position) per cell of source-median-split identity and position margins, maximizing empirical net correctness with one pseudo-observation favoring stop by each action's fee. Source Samantha/Joelle, neighbors biko/nelo, 233/253 wpm. Freeze before reading held outcomes. Held Rishi/Tessa, neighbors dupo/saro, 229/249 wpm, equal placement of differing word first/last, endings fully crossed. All reported interventions share original utterance/fees. Primary per voice: ≥.025 net/output over strongest development-selected constant (including always asking inferred position), ≥.05 gain vs suppressing paid content, ≥75% correct position among targeted purchases; report actual query counts vs total opportunities, and compare spending-matched shuffled timing and fixed-position questions. No claim of inducing question syntax: environment supplies positional request and scripted spoken answer.

Source fit selected the inferred-slot question in all four cells at .10; therefore it cannot beat the same fixed inquiry policy on the primary comparison. Before held scoring, add separately labelled pricing forks: multiply both fees by 2 and 3.5, refit only on source counterfactual outcomes at each price, compare same-price source-selected constant and report every test decision. No change to primary analysis. Compute the test-set break-even fee for asking the inferred-slot question on every item versus stopping as the raw paired correctness difference.

The primary learner asked every time, choosing the acoustically inferred disputed position correctly on 64/64 utterances per voice, balanced 32 first and 32 last. Bare-name replies corrected all 12 original Rishi errors and all eight Tessa errors, with zero spoiled decisions. Primary gross 64/64 each, net .900/output vs stop .8125/.875 and always slower repeat .708125/.801875. Suppressing paid content at the same fee reduced net to .7125/.775, meeting the content and position criteria. No adaptive advantage vs the preregistered constant: both are exactly the same action, as is a timing shuffle with the same action counts. Crucially all original errors occurred when the disputed noun was second; always asking for the LAST word also achieved 64/64 and .900 on both voices, while always asking first achieved net .7125/.775. Thus location was acoustically recovered but choosing a varying location had no extra score over that shortcut on these placements. Example: Rishi's dupo-ka gavi-ka at 229 wpm initially selected tavi. Both acoustic completions preferred the differing name in second position, the requested bare reply decoded as gavi, and asking first instead kept the original wrong answer.

Measured all-ask vs stop break-even fee .1875 Rishi and .125 Tessa per reply for these distributions. At .20 the source model still asked always (net .800 both); at .35 it bought 14/64 and 15/64, losing .02969 and .01953 net/output to its source-selected stop control. At .10 a hindsight policy knowing the initial mistakes could reach .98125/.9875; this gap is not evidence of publicly predictable mistakes. No selective inquiry improvement demonstrated. Two synthetic voices, 64 distinct initial recordings per voice, a slower repeat shared across the two initial rates and a small reused bare reply vocabulary. Modeled fees omit historical training, true human effort and runtime. Source fitting used evaluator replay of all four actions on 128 labelled source inputs. Registered acoustic operations ran locally; utterance generation, offered request meanings and response policy were supplied externally. Verified all held actions by reconstructing candidate costs, normalized fusion, chosen slot, decoded receipt and counterfactual net directly from stored observations. ### Clarifying speaker intent when either word can be acoustically ambiguous

Pair clean recordings differing only in gavi/tavi. Add their mono PCM at equal individual RMS after zero-padding ends to a common length; the exact same combined waveform is used in two balanced branches with opposite intended names and a truthful solo reply. This is a constructed collision, not naturally ambiguous human speech. Cross clean and collision at equal prevalence, both word positions and both endings; reuse original word-request source and target panels/receipts. Neither mixture status nor intended speaker is supplied. Choice uses only log best template cost, absolute normalized identity margin and absolute normalized position margin, standardized on source, with a regularized logistic estimate of correctness for each action. Fit all four counterfactual action outcomes on Samantha/Joelle; evaluate frozen on Rishi/Tessa. At primary prices .20 for a bare word and .24 for repeat, compare stop, always repeat, always inferred disputed position, fixed first/last and exact expected spending-matched shuffle. Require ≥.015 net/output over best applicable constant (including always inferred slot), paid and unpaid decisions on ≥10% each, and ≥.05 gain after a paid request vs withheld reply at unchanged fee, per voice. Report matched receipts and both-intentions-correct on identical collision audio, counts split by requested slot and cleaner recordings, attainable vs actual utility. Separately check .05/.45 bare prices at fixed proportional repeat fee; no further model fitting.

At the primary fee the learner always requested the inferred slot on both held voices. Diagnostic on opened trials: compare an evaluator knowing only clean vs collision and acting by ask-on-collision, stop-on-clean, against a source-trained logistic detector using the same three public features but explicit clean/collision labels during source fitting (threshold .5). Report detection and downstream returns separately. The public clean/collision distinction may have very little economic headroom even with a perfect detector.

The listener located the disputed position on 128/128 branches per voice, 64 first and 64 last. It bought that slot on all 128 at .20. All 32 ambiguous waveform pairs per voice were correct in both opposite-intention branches after the spoken reply. On the ambiguous branches alone, stopping necessarily scored 32/64, with 16 errors in each position. Including clean speech, stop scored 84/128 Rishi and 88/128 Tessa; targeted clarification 128/128 on both. Always asking last scored 112/128 gross on both (net .675); always first scored 100/128 and 104/128 (.58125/.6125). Thus varying the requested position demonstrably helped over either fixed position on this constructed workload. Withholding paid replies reduced learned net by .34375 and .3125. Primary policy tied the stronger always-inferred-slot control and the spending-matched shuffle at net .8; missed selective stopping and superiority criteria. Whole repeat net .5881/.6272 and hindsight payoff with known errors .93125/.9375. At .05 it still always asked; at .45 it asked on 18/128 Rishi (net .60078 vs stop .65625) and zero Tessa (.6875). Neither supplementary price established superiority.

The source-supervised collision detector identified only 16/64 ambiguous held branches on each voice with zero false positives on 64 clean ones. Net .69375/.725 when used as a gate. An evaluator explicitly given collision status, asking only there, reached .80625/.83750: only .00625 headroom over always asking on Rishi and .0375 on Tessa under this simple routing. Both identifiability of useful uncertainty and achievable cost savings matter. Acoustic ambiguity is synthetic: two overlapping normalized name variants are mixed, and a scripted teacher knows which one was intended. Counts reuse two intentions on 32 shared mixtures plus 64 clean recordings per voice. No autonomous dialogue, natural noise or improved default policy claimed. All four-action choices and normalized fusion reconstructed independently from stored raw scores and decoded receipts; paired mixtures had identical sample hashes and costs across intentions. External collision_clarify.py VOICE (source), collision_clarify.py (fit), collision_clarify.py VOICE (held), collision_clarify_check.py (three-fee evaluation) and collision_detect.py under mac.sh.

### Grounding which earlier spoken item a clarification cue requests

Move the meaning of the clarification request into the experiment. Two spoken cue forms (existing anonymous acoustic handles 0 and 7) exchange conventions across matched speakers: request the second item or the last. Candidate meanings supplied as signed ordinal indices ±1 through ±4, invalid uses eliminated. No explicit slot labels in teaching. First hear a two-item phrase and a recorded teacher reply for each cue, where both meanings select the same name. Additional lesson uses another two-item phrase (redundant) or a three-item phrase where the replies differ. Filter each cue's programs by equality between heard reply and selected heard item; do not force meanings to be distinct. If surviving programs predict different names at test, abstain. Freeze policies after teaching; reload before testing 16 fresh four-item recordings in eight matched second/last transpositions. Same query audio across conventions, same passage across cue/reply interventions. Primary known-length acoustic arm (supplied word count only); compare free-length decoding, exact-hearing diagnostic, one/redundant/true/swapped grounding and cue swap. Require ≥6 of 8 complete transposition sets per voice (all eight combinations of passage reversal, cue and convention correct), ≥20-point gain vs redundant lesson and ≥90% single decisions. Report what is identifiable before teaching and after masking the cue. No claim of syntax induction or open-ended dialogue. Synthetic voices Rishi/Tessa, established frozen registered sequence reader. Source of ordinal functions, isolated lexical enrollment, prerecorded teacher replies and list framing explicitly supplied.

Both voices met all primary criteria: 8/8 complete matched transposition sets and 64/64 name decisions on 16 four-item recordings per voice, both with supplied length and with the frozen prior length penalty over 1–6 possible words. Rishi's full passage was correct on 15/16 recordings and Tessa's on 16/16; the one misheard item was outside the requested positions. All three teaching passages and all spoken cue/reply identities decoded correctly. After one or two two-item lessons, both cues retained the two possible indices {second,last}; all 64 fresh decisions properly abstained at zero additional teaching. One three-item passage with one reply for each cue resolved both mappings independently. Swapping only those informative replies between conventions, or swapping the two heard cues, gave 0/64 instead of 64/64 with all items otherwise fixed. With neither cue identity available, neither unique answer was justified. Exact-symbol diagnostics matched the acoustic decisions. The matched-world vocabulary supports this causal interpretation, not a claim of 64 independent conversations. Each learner received four spoken teacher answers in the informative two-passage condition. No additional target teaching or tuning.

Example recording: the two names kemi mepo followed by pavo → mepo leave both positions possible. The three names mepo zilu vashi followed by pavo → zilu ground second. On luno mepo kemi zilu the frozen reader consequently answers mepo. The other convention on the same passage/cue asks for zilu. Reproduction: ordinal_request.py VOICE fit/test, ordinal_request_check.py and ordinal_request_replay.py via mac.sh in the local dual-role-word archive. Separate fresh test processes reload fitted programs. Available eight-form inventory, potential positional operations, passage/cue/teacher turn boundaries and filtering rule were supplied; cue-to-operation mapping and actual passage reading came from acoustic evidence. No free-form question generation, independent human teacher or deployment change. Verified matched transpositions, distinct held waveform hashes, all finite-program responses, abstentions, and the replay's PCM format.

Independent iterative target evaluation and exact-symbol diagnostic matched the acoustic predictions. PCM replay checks verified identical nonzero sample streams across the paired queries, with only silence placement changed. Interpretation is conditional on supplied binary event semantics, alternating token types, possible pause policies, and an existing mechanism to apply the policy recursively. Two prerecorded synthetic voices with deliberately separated word units; no natural prosody or human evaluation. External pause_attachment.py VOICE fit/test and pause_attach_check.py via mac.sh save receiver fits, test traces, secondary probes and pause-attach-example-VOICE.wav.

### Grounding whether pitch or pause carries a grouping contrast

Freeze two miniature conventions, one groups at the longest preceding gap, the other at the highest-pitched operator relative to that word's enrollment. Candidates also include shortest/lowest and fixed endpoints. Use paired three-name teaching queries: first both cues agree (both rank orders), then a conflicting utterance with a distinguishing answer. Test 16 independent four-name placements seed 217412, all four combinations of pitch and pause rank reversal, exact same PCM across conventions. Reuse the prior lexical model and eight isolated TTS sounds; pitch-shift only operator words by −4/0/+4 semitones (two-level training ±4), using resampling and tempo compensation, preserve sample counts and normalize RMS. Receiver estimates voiced fundamental from normalized time-domain autocorrelation, relative to isolated same-word enrollment. No true word identity, renderer pitch setting or timed boundaries given to receiver. Require ≥13/16 complete crossed sets per voice and ≥20-point conflicting-cue gain over either fixed cue. Separately score lexical identity, inferred gap/pitch ranks and semantic performance with oracle tokens vs oracle prosody. Controlled resynthesis, possible timbral artifacts and presegmented source audio are limitations; reported generalization concerns cue use, not naturally produced intonation.

Primary failed before semantic fitting: both hypothesis pools emptied. Neither voice decoded a complete held token string (0/64), despite correct gap ordering on 64/64. At 183 wpm both pitch-shifted operators in the very first teaching question were heard as name tokens. Frozen-pool oracle-at-test controls are also zero because a later oracle cannot restore a lost fit. Separate repair: add four pitch-shifted isolated operator enrollments at 173 wpm (both signs × both operators), no new names or source answers. Retain the failed arm, refit same teaching design and reserve fresh four-name placements seed 217413, at 211 wpm. Add diagnostic refits using exact tokens during teaching and testing with measured pitch/gaps, or true prosodic ranks with actual token readings. No further held-out tuning.

With four additional isolated enrollments per voice, both heard all 64 new recordings' words and recovered all 64 pitch rank orders. Correlated teaching retained longest-gap and highest-pitch as possible in BOTH communities. One conflicting answered example per community selected its intended cue. Frozen listening on the repair cohort: 16/16 complete sets, 128/128 branches per voice, including 64/64 conflicting-cue branches versus 32/64 for each fixed cue. On identical repair-cohort audio the original bank identified 0/64 full strings, but a refit using exact word identities with measured pitch and gaps recovered 128/128. This locates the original binding failure in lexical recognition under synthetic pitch variation. Pitch-shifted operator arrays have the same sample count at each pitch; normalized pitch is computed from the enrolled identity. The manipulated waveform may carry timbre artifacts; the cue score itself uses estimated fundamental, and this controlled design does not demonstrate natural intonation comprehension. The original 16 placements required 162 draws. Independent checks verified shared observations across conventions and endpoint denominators. External pitch_pause.py VOICE fit/test [repaired|lexoracle|rankoracle], pitch_pause_check.py and pitch_repair_check.py via mac.sh; repaired paired WAV pitch-pause-example-repaired-VOICE.wav has identical intended pauses and different operator pitch placements.

### Transferring learned acoustic categories to real held-out human speakers

New local corpus: Jakobovski et al., Free Spoken Digit Dataset (FSDD), https://github.com/Jakobovski/free-spoken-digit-dataset, pinned revision 26eb9aaf76e81b692f806f9140c2d2777410d7a1. Six speakers, ten isolated English digit words; here labels are anonymous acoustic category handles. Preregister all six leave-one-speaker-out folds, primary test repetition indices 0–4 per word, secondary 40–49 untouched panel. Matched source budget 100 labeled recordings: five donors × two repetitions (10,11) per word vs one donor × ten (10–19), exhaust all five possible single donors and average their accuracies without using held speaker to pick a winner. No pretrained model, target feedback or waveform metadata as input. Mono PCM resampled from 8 to 16 kHz before registered speech-envelope, speech-fit and speech-match. Separate target adaptation: two labeled examples per word (5,6), comparing source bank plus 20 local exemplars to local-only and same-source-budget +20 donor tokens. Target ≥75% pooled zero-shot accuracy and ≥10-point diverse-minus-single mean gain with improvements on ≥5/6 speakers. Report each speaker, label budgets, macro errors and paired uncertainty clustered by speaker; no claim to natural intonation, number semantics or continuous speech. No test-time choice among arms.

All 3,000 corpus files downloaded outside Git and hash-unique (FSDD, CC BY-SA 4.0). Primary zero-target accuracy 159/300 (53.0%) versus 49.07% mean of equal-budget single-donor arms; paired speaker-cluster bootstrap gain +3.93 points [−2.47,+8.80], 5/6 speaker wins. Neither pooled accuracy nor effect-size threshold passed. Diverse primary per-person correct /50: george 21, jackson 34, lucas 27, nicolas 22, theo 31, yweweler 24. With two target examples per category, mixed+local 276/300 (92.0%), local-only 275/300 (91.67%); adding 20 more donor examples instead 169/300. Secondary 600 distinct clips: mixed 323/600 vs 50.83% single mean; +3.0 points [−4.2,+11.3], wins 4/6. Mixed+local and local-only both 551/600; augmented donor-only 346/600. Most common primary errors 0→2 and 3→6 (14 each), also top two secondary (22 and 27). Variation includes possible speaker, accent, microphone and session effects; these six people do not disentangle causes. Results show rapid supervised enrollment of already covered categories, weak general transfer in this reader, and negligible benefit over local-only after complete enrollment. No conclusions on category meanings, target-unheard words or spontaneous conversation. Local human_digit.py SPEAKER primary/secondary and human_digit_check.py via mac.sh; speaker-fold raw results and pinned corpus saved locally. No recordings committed or published.

### Does human voice calibration transfer to words the speaker never demonstrated?

Freeze all six FSDD speaker folds and same 100 donor examples as preceding study. Split categories by parity and exchange halves; in each fold allow two target recordings (indices 5/6) for five words only. Primary test indices 20–29, independent second block 30–39. Always predict among all ten handles; each clip is scored once as unseen and once as demonstrated across counterbalanced installations. Primary registered speech-fit-voice maps each donor using ten same-category time-normalized calibration pairs; keep transformed donor exemplars only. Compare unchanged donor bank, adding ten local examples directly, a donor-specific mean offset estimated from the same balanced word samples, and registered mapping with cyclically mismatched calibration words. Require ≥10-point unheard-category gain vs unchanged, positive lower paired 95% speaker-level bootstrap bound and wins on ≥5/6 individuals on each block. Separately require ≥5-point gain over mean-offset to credit detailed alignment beyond broad speaker/channel normalization. Report direct target-word performance, outsider prediction errors, outcomes by person and marginal teaching. Held speakers and word identities were used in prior studies but these test recordings were not scored in those runs. No attribution of channel effects to anatomy or phonology.

On target-unheard categories, mapped vs unchanged: 416/600 vs 324/600 (69.3% vs 54.0%; +15.3 points, speaker bootstrap [10.0,21.3]) and 400/600 vs 312/600 (66.7% vs 52.0%; +14.7 [8.5,20.8]). All six people improved on both blocks; each 600-clip score has one evaluation per recording, each speaker contributed 100 unheard-category clips over the two complementary installations. Target-unheard primary/secondary mapped counts per speaker out of 100: george 58/40, jackson 85/90, lucas 66/63, nicolas 52/55, theo 81/80, yweweler 74/72. Mean-offset alone got 406 and 393, leaving mapped gains +1.7 [−2.7,5.7] and +1.2 [−1.3,4.0] points, below the 5-point criterion. Wrong word pairing got 348 and 343. Appending ten local exemplars got 269/253 on unheard categories but 559/545 on directly taught categories (mapped 524/486). Of mapped errors on unheard words, 66/184 and 81/200 landed on taught labels, remainder on other unheard categories. Both blocks use the same six people, not independent population replications. Cross-word calibration transfer was demonstrated in this corpus; the specific advantage over a mean acoustic correction was not. No direct examples of the five target-probe categories in each installation's local bank; ten-category choice kept open. Local human_half.py SPEAKER and human_half_check.py via mac.sh reproduce results from pinned local FSDD. Verified per-recording identity, balanced complementary exposure, complete denominators and separate performance on directly taught vs held target words.

### Reading whole utterances recorded by a human without word timestamps

OpenSLR SLR1 Yesno (https://www.openslr.org/1/), anonymous Kaldi contributor, 60 actual Hebrew eight-word recordings from one speaker, 8 kHz, archive SHA-256 c3f49e0cca421f96b75b41640749167b52118f232498667ca7a5f9416aef8e73. No English-digit or synthetic examples used. Split by lexicographic order of WAV content SHA-256, first 8 fit, next 12 development, final 40 held. Supervise ordered binary category handles only; word time boundaries absent. At training derive eight segments from low-energy intervals (or equal-width fallback); at test infer segment count from waveform only. Registered envelope, speech-fit and speech-match; resample to 16 kHz. Choose RMS threshold {0.015,0.035,0.07} of peak and silent gap {60,100,140,180} ms on development exact sequences, then edit distance, fixed grid order for ties. Freeze before held run. Compare fixed eight-segment counterpart, fixed equal eighths, and per-position majority; report free-decoding whole sequences, substitutions/deletions/insertions by minimum edit alignment and count accuracy. Require ≥32/40 complete fresh sequences and ≥20 points over equal-width segmentation. Score 1/4/8 transcript ablations at same selected settings; count development supervision separately. Timing annotations unavailable; segmentation diagnostics measure count and outcome effects rather than true timestamp accuracy. No language semantics or speaker transfer claim.

Development selected the 7%-of-peak threshold and 60 ms minimum gap, with 6/12 exact sequences. With eight labeled fit recordings, free-count recognition scored 16/40 exact on held recordings, with the correct word count on 30/40 and 47 edits in 320 words (30 substitutions, 5 deletions, 12 insertions). Both primary criteria failed. Fixed eight longest-gap segments gave 18/40 exact and 40 edits, equal eighths gave 24/40 and 27 edits, and positional majority gave 0/40. Free-count recognition using the nested one- and four-recording fit subsets gave 29/40 and 8/40 respectively. These subsets do not estimate the average effect of additional teaching. On the opened panel, holding free test cuts fixed and using equal eighths for training instead raised exact recognition from 16 to 27 and reduced edits from 47 to 22. Combining equal training cuts and forced-count test cuts gave 30/40 with 19 edits. The enrollment segmentation materially affects recognition; timestamps are unavailable to identify which cuts were correct. Verified all 60 distinct recording hashes and the saved target sequences. One speaker, two words, eight tokens per recording; ordered transcripts supplied for eight fit and twelve model-selection recordings. Local registered acoustic operations and external segmentation/scoring. Reproduce with hebrew_sequence.py and hebrew_sequence_check.py through mac.sh in the external dual-role-word archive.

### Aligning recurring pronunciations to ordered human transcripts

Freeze one leave-one-recording-out alignment pass over the original eight Hebrew teaching recordings, starting from equal eighths. Permit equal, free-gap and longest-gap boundaries within 0.8 mean word durations of each nominal internal cut. Find the ordered path minimizing summed same-category acoustic distance to other recordings. Compare equal training, duration-only and category-blind acoustic alignment under identical free-count test cuts. Use the twelve development recordings then report the previously opened forty only as a regression. Require at least four more complete sequences than equal training there and improvement over category-blind alignment before crediting category-specific alignment. No test transcripts used to place cuts.

Equal/duration/category-blind/category-aware alignments yielded 10/10/9/10 complete development sequences of 12, with 3/3/4/3 edits. On the opened 40-recording regression all four got 27 exact, with 22/22/25/25 edits. Category-aware alignment changed 33 of 56 internal training boundaries, recovered one sequence but broke another. Independently verified contiguous eight-segment paths, unchanged testing counts, and paired switches. Of the 13 equal-training failures, ten had the wrong segment count. No evidence of improvement. Reproduce with hebrew_align.py via mac.sh; per-mode paths and readings saved externally.

Freeze a second, listening-time comparison. Keep equal-cut enrollment. Infer a variable number of words by searching low-energy candidate boundaries (at least 20 ms inactivity); assign each segment the nearest enrolled pronunciation and use the fitted median training duration as a soft prior on length. Limit individual segments to 0.3–1.9 times the training median. Compare duration-only search to duration plus squared envelope distance (weight one, no fitting on the opened 40). Normalize the sum by elapsed time using segment duration weights. Test the ordinary recordings and concatenated nonoverlapping pairs in hash order, including the join inside the continuous search. No expected token count or transcript at recognition. Concatenated 16-word recordings are engineered stress controls. Require ≥32/40 exact single sequences and at least three more than duration-only for evidence of a benefit from joint acoustic search.

On development singles both duration-only and joint search achieved 11/12; both failed to find a legal path on all six joined pairs. On regression singles both reached 28/40 exact, with 42/41 edits and four missing paths each (missing path scored as all deletions). On the 20 joined pairs, both got zero exact and 17 missing paths. Joining intact clips can put extended silence inside a search whose maximum word duration includes silence. Post-primary diagnostic: compress each interior below-threshold run longer than 200 ms to 200 ms, rerun unchanged. On regression singles duration-only/joint reached 33/32 of 40, with 38 edits each and four missing paths; on pairs 13/10 of 20, with 55/57 edits and three missing paths each. Development singles 10/9 of 12 and pairs 5/3 of 6. Verified monotone output paths and counts from saved decisions. Accounting for long pauses helps on artificial joins; the acoustic term did not beat duration-only. Four failed single paths alone account for 32 of the 38 residual edits. No proof of naturally varying-length comprehension, fresh-speaker transfer, or superiority on independent recordings. External hebrew_live_align.py and hebrew_live_pauses.py via mac.sh, with raw paths archived; no default decoder change.

### Preserving a partial hearing when a segment cannot be placed

Freeze the previous duration-only listener, equal-cut enrollment and silence compression. At each pair of consecutive candidate cuts allow an unrecognized interval. Minimize total skipped samples first and the previous duration penalty second; emit no word for a skipped interval and retain its coordinates. Compare to the previous strict search and a fallback that switches the entire failed recording to the original free-gap reader. Evaluate unchanged on the opened 40 single recordings and their 20 concatenations. Require unchanged readings on every previous complete path and at least halved aggregate edit counts on each panel. Test exact digital silence and paired silence insertion at the join. Word omissions and insertions remain charged. No independent speaker or new corpus in this regression.

Freeze separate English-digit transfer before running: reuse the Hebrew policies unchanged, with local word templates and median duration enrolled from FSDD isolated clips 5 and 6 per category per speaker (duration measured after trimming envelope silence by the previous RMS rule). Construct 30 passages per speaker, 6 each of length 2–6, seed 63491, shuffle 120 distinct probe clips per speaker from indices 20–39, uniformly randomized joins of 0/80/240 ms. No true count, join positions or category during recognition. Provide an isolated-word classifier on exact joins as diagnostic, charge skipped spans, report by speaker. This tests transfer of boundary handling to a different corpus with independently recorded words, not spontaneous English or unseen voices with no enrollment.

Hebrew opened regression: complete sequences strict/partial/fallback each 33/40 singles and 13/20 pairs. Word edits 38/17/13 on singles and 55/15/14 on pairs. All previously complete paths preserved their readings. Partial recovery passed both halving thresholds but whole-recording fallback was stronger. Development 10/12 and 5/6 exact for all three arms. Digital silence emitted nothing; one second of added silence preserved the partial output in 19/20 regression pairs and 6/6 development pairs. Silence invariance therefore incomplete.

English transfer, strict/partial/fallback/exact-join classifier, exact passages of 30 followed by word edits in 120 words: George 14/14/14/16 and 25/22/22/16; Jackson 21/21/21/25 and 15/11/12/5; Lucas 7/7/7/27 and 47/43/42/3; Nicolas 4/4/4/11 and 79/64/59/27; Theo 4/4/9/28 and 80/59/45/3; Yweweler 6/6/8/22 and 70/45/35/11. Totals 56/56/63/129 exact of 180, with 316/244/215/65 edits in 720 words. An additional always-free-gap control reached 61 exact, 218 edits. Verified per-passage fallback choices by reconstructing the audio and rerunning free-gap recognition. Partial recovery reduced edits on every speaker but restored no whole passages. Timing and acoustic boundary placement remain limiting even with isolated-word training from each tested speaker. Same passage recipes across voices; lexical recordings unique per speaker and disjoint from these training clips, but the speakers and corpus are previously studied. No source or target word meanings learned here. Local reproduction via hebrew_partial.py, human_partial.py SPEAKER and human_partial_check.py under mac.sh in dual-role-word. Full traces saved outside Git.

### Calibrating word boundaries from isolated human speech examples

Freeze FSDD digit clips 5 and 6 per word and speaker as the only calibration audio; make artificial multiword practice by leaving one index out for recognition and assembling the other into 24 two-to-five-word examples in each direction, seed 74102, joins 0/80/240 ms. Search peak-relative RMS levels .02/.04/.07/.12 and gaps 20/40/60/100 ms using total transcript edits, then more exact sequences and finally grid order. No word-count or joins provided at inference. Compare the speaker-specific choice, the choice pooled across the other five speakers, the fixed .07/60 ms setting, and exact-join recognition with the same two-example acoustic bank. Test on 30 fresh arrangements per speaker, 2–6 words evenly, 120 distinct clips from indices 7–19, seed 74103; label these new passages of known speakers. Prespecify ≥10 percentage points more exact than fixed, ≥5 points more than pooled and positive speaker-paired bootstrap lower bound for the latter. Separately report full word errors and count accuracy. The identical source clips support both acoustic enrollment and calibration. This supplies word isolation during teaching and models pauses by construction.

Fresh passage results, exact / edits / correct count (180 passages, 720 words): fixed 61/209/107, speaker-specific 67/184/109, pooled other speakers 68/183/124, exact-join diagnostic 142/49/180. Speaker-specific exact by person George/Jackson/Lucas/Nicolas/Theo/Yweweler = 6/22/15/6/7/11 versus fixed 14/19/12/5/3/8. Paired mean improvement over fixed +3.33 percentage points, six-speaker bootstrap 95% interval [−9.44,+11.11]; over pooled −0.56 [−11.67,+8.33]. Both thresholds missed. The same pooled setting (.12,60 ms) won every leave-one-speaker-out fit. No use of target transcripts in selecting these settings. Totals verified from raw predictions, unique test clip identities, disjoint fit indices and reconstruction of expected sequences. Same passage contents/relative pause assignments across voices, six participants, corpus already studied.

Post-primary causal comparison using the same word recordings in each of the 180 passages: put either zero or 240 ms of additional digital silence at EVERY join, hold fitted speaker-specific policies fixed. Exact 63 vs 96; 39 wrong-to-right and 6 right-to-wrong. Correct token count 100 vs 136, substitutions/deletions/insertions 108/81/34 vs 71/0/53. Longer pauses eliminated deletion errors under the fixed edit tie rule but left 84 incomplete/incorrect passages.
